5.2.25.1. Description of Conditions
        This subsection discusses simple and compound conditions.

Simple Conditions
        There are six types of conditions that can be evaluated as true or false. For
        complete syntax and detailed explanations, refer to 5.2.25.2 through 5.2.25.7.

        Condition 1
        Test if a numeric variable is nonzero or zero (see 5.2.25.2).


                 −    IS              
                                     SET

                                     CLEAR

        Condition 2
        Test if satisfying a bracketed reference results in a NO FIND (see 5.2.25.3).

         expression

        Condition 3
        Test if a numeric expression is nonzero or zero (see 5.2.25.4).

         expression

        Condition 4
        Compare two operands symbolically or numerically (see 5.2.25.5).

                         ⎧<=⎫
                           <
                         ⎪=⎪
              −1                      −2
                         ⎨<>⎬
                         ⎪>=⎪
                         ⎩>⎭

        Condition 5
        Search selected SGSs, PCF sets, or TCF sets for a symbolic value (see 5.2.25.6).


                            ASC
         COLUMN  SEARCH ('          () FROM   ), *, +, ,- FOR  
          ROW               ASCENDING
                            DESC
          KEYWORD
                            DESCENDING




5–32                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                                 SSG Directives




         Condition 6
         Test if an element has permanent or temporary corrections (see 5.2.25.7).


                    (/   −    ) HAS ANY          ) COR(RECTIONS)
                                                           PERM
                     −                                   (
                                                           TEMP

         The following applies to evaluation of all six conditions:

              Failure to satisfy references

               A failed attempt to satisfy any reference immediately results in the entire
               condition being set to false. No further examination of the condition is performed.
               The variable NOFND$ is not incremented. The normal NO FIND error messages
               are not output during condition evaluation. None of the following error messages
               is output:

                 *ERROR* - NO FIND BEFORE COLUMN c ON FOLLOWING IMAGE
                 Variable ‘variable’ is undefined.
                 SGS reference ‘[reference]’ is undefined.

              Format errors
               Format errors result in the normal error messages, in ERRCNT$ being incremented
               and in the entire condition being set to false. No further examination of the
               condition is performed.

              Supplying condition keywords
               The condition syntax includes the keywords IS, SET, CLEAR, VALUE, OF, <, <=,
               =, <>, >=, >, ROW, COLUMN, KEYWORD, SEARCH, ASC, ASCENDING, DESC,
               DESCENDING, FROM, FOR, HAS, ANY, PERM, TEMP, COR, and CORRECTIONS.
               Strings, variable references, or process parameter references that return the
               symbolic keyword may supply all of these. If a NO FIND results when you
               attempt to satisfy the references, the condition is set to false.

              Boolean keywords within conditions
               The keywords AND, OR, and XOR are Boolean operators described below for
               compound conditions. Incorrect evaluation of the condition or a format error
               may result if a string, variable reference, or process parameter reference returns
               a Boolean operator for any part of the condition. The Boolean operators may
               appear within a condition if they are supplied by an SGS reference or a set
               reference.




7831 1784–010                                                                                 5–33
SSG Directives




Compound Conditions
         The *IF and *ELSEIF directives can test more than one condition by use of compound
         conditions, also called logical or Boolean expressions. The compound conditions
         evaluate as true or false using one or more conditions and the logical or Boolean
         operators NOT, AND, OR, and XOR.

         Format


            NOT    − 1 (OR    NOT    − 2 (OR  … ))
                                 AND                         AND

                                 XOR                         XOR

            where:
              condition-n            Is any one of the six types of simple conditions.
              NOT, AND, OR, XOR      Are the Boolean operators. See “Results of
                                     Boolean Operations.”
              parentheses ( )        Used to indicate an optional entry in the syntax
                                     of a compound condition, as in (NOT).
                                     Can also be used to change the evaluation order
                                     of Boolean expressions. See “Using Parenthetical
                                     Expressions to Change Evaluation Order” and
                                     also “Constraints Against Using Parenthetical
                                     Expressions.”



         Description
         This subsection describes in more detail the syntactical components for compound
         conditions.

         Results of Boolean Operations

         Table 5–2 contains the possible combinations of conditions and the true/false results
         produced by the Boolean operators.


                        Table 5–2. Results of Boolean Operations
                             Using AND, OR, XOR, and NOT


                                         Result       Result        Result           NOT
       Condition-1     Condition-2       of AND       of OR         of XOR        Condition-1

          false             false         false        false         false           true
          false             true          false         true         true            true
           true             false         false         true         true            false
           true             true          true          true         false           false




5–34                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                               SSG Directives




         Priorities of Boolean Operators

         Table 5–3 shows the priorities of the Boolean operators, where 1 is the highest priority
         and 3 is the lowest. High priority operators are evaluated before low priority operators
         and operators with the same priority are evaluated from left to right.

         Traditionally SSG has assigned the same priority to AND, OR, and XOR (see column 2
         of this table). Beginning with SSG 24R1, it is possible to have SSG assign a higher
         priority to AND than to OR and XOR, as is customary for most languages that support
         Boolean operations (see column 3 of Table 5–3).


                        Table 5–3. Boolean Operator Priorities


                                       Traditional            New
                                       Evaluation          Evaluation
                        Operator         Priority           Priority

                          NOT               1                  1
                          AND               2                  2
                           OR               2                  3
                          XOR               2                  3


         Using NEW_BOOLEAN$ to Control Evaluation Priorities

         Skeletons must be designed to operate with either the traditional or the new
         evaluation priorities, but not with both. The reserved global variable NEW_BOOLEAN$
         controls which evaluation priorities are to be used during skeleton execution.

            When NEW_BOOLEAN$ has the default value of zero, the traditional evaluation
             priorities are used, and skeletons will continue to operate as they always have.
            When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is explicitly set to a nonzero value, the new evaluation
             priorities are used.
         Note: You must set NEW_BOOLEAN$ at the beginning of skeleton execution,
         because once a NOT, AND, OR, or XOR operator is encountered, NEW_BOOLEAN$
         is set to read-only and cannot be changed for the remainder of the skeleton
         execution.

         Using Parenthetical Expressions to Change Evaluation Order

         When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, left and right parentheses can be used to bracket
         any simple or compound condition, thereby creating a parenthetical expression. A
         parenthetical expression can be used to change the order in which Boolean
         expressions are evaluated. The evaluation of a Boolean expression may be interrupted
         to evaluate a parenthetical expression contained within it. This might produce an
         evaluation order that differs from the one shown in Table 5–3.




7831 1784–010                                                                               5–35
SSG Directives




           Format of a parenthetical expression
                Left parentheses are only allowed to the left of a NOT Boolean operator
                 and to the left of a simple condition.
                Right parentheses are only allowed to the right of a simple condition.
                Each left parenthesis must have a matching right parenthesis.
                To improve readability, any number of space characters may be included
                 before and after parentheses.
           Attributes of a parenthetical expression
                Evaluates as true or false.
                Evaluates as a separate unit.
                Allowed anywhere a simple condition is allowed.
                May be nested up to 25 levels.
        When a parenthesis is encountered, the reserved global variable NEW_BOOLEAN$
        is set to read-only and cannot be changed for the remainder of the skeleton execution.

        Constraints Against Using Parentheses

           Parentheses are not allowed within simple conditions.
            Simple condition formats 2, 3, 4, and 5, described in 5.2.25.3, 5.2.25.4, 5.2.25.5,
            and 5.2.25.6, respectively, all allow numeric expressions such as VALUE1-VALUE2-
            VALUE3. The use of parentheses in a numeric expression such as VALUE1-
            (VALUE2+VALUE3) is not allowed and results in a format error.

           Parentheses are not allowed within any bracketed reference.
            Bracketed references may contain numeric expressions, but parentheses within a
            numeric expression are not allowed and will result in a format error. For example,
            The -A-B-C in [LABEL,-A-B-C,1,1] is valid, but [LABEL,-(A+B+C),1,1] is not.

        Compatibility Issues When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is Nonzero

           Parentheses are special characters for the new evaluation. *IF directives that
            contain parentheses may operate when NEW_BOOLEAN$ is zero, but not when
            it is nonzero.
                Strings that contain parentheses might cause format errors, as in this
                 example:
                  *IF COLUMN SEARCH FROM TEXT,1,1,1 FOR Copyright(c)

                If variable names, SGS labels, PCF set names, or TCF set names contain left
                 or right parentheses, they may cause format errors, as in this example:
                  *SET SUB(1)TOT = A+B+C+D+E
                  *SET SUB(2)TOT = I+J+K+L+M
                  *SET SUB(3)TOT = V+W+X+Y+Z
                  *IF SUB(1)TOT+SUB(2)TOT < +SUB(3)TOT




5–36                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                                    SSG Directives




         Evaluating Boolean Expressions Without Parentheses

         The following steps describe the procedure for both the traditional evaluation of a
         Boolean expression (NEW_BOOLEAN$ = 0) and the new evaluation of a Boolean
         expression that does not include parentheses (NEW_BOOLEAN$ = nonzero).

         1.   Evaluate condition-1 as true or false and save it as result-1. If the optional NOT
              operator precedes condition-1, negate result-1 (as shown in Table 5–2). If result-1
              is true, change it to false. If result-1 is false, change it to true.
         2.   If this is the end of the expression, proceed to step 6. Otherwise, this should be
              an AND, OR, or XOR operator, which is saved as operator-1.
              a.   If result-1 is false and this is the AND operator, store false in result-2. Ignore
                   the optional NOT operator and condition-2 that follow.
              b. If result-1 is true and this is the OR operator, store true in result-2. Ignore the
                 optional NOT operator and condition-2 that follow.
              c.   Otherwise, evaluate condition-2 as true or false and save it as result-2.
                   If the optional NOT operator precedes condition-2, negate result-2.
         3.   If this is the end of the expression, proceed to step 5. Otherwise, this should be
              an AND, OR, or XOR operator.
              a.   If traditional evaluation is in use, evaluate the expression “result-1 operator-1
                   result-2” (as shown in Table 5–2). Store the evaluation result in result-1 and
                   return to step 2 to process this operator as the new operator-1.

              b. If new evaluation is in use and the priority of operator-1 is the same as or
                 higher than the priority of this operator, then evaluate the expression “result-1
                 operator-1 result-2” and store the evaluation result in result-1. Return to step
                 2 to process this operator as the new operator-1.

              c.   If new evaluation is in use and the priority of operator-1 is lower than the
                   priority of this operator, then store this operator as operator-2. This can only
                   occur if operator-1 is a low priority OR or XOR operator and operator-2 is a
                   high priority AND operator. Proceed to step 4.

         4. If new evaluation is in use, process the condition following the AND operator as
            follows:
              a.   If result-2 is false, ignore the optional NOT operator and condition-3 that
                   follow. Return to step 3.
              b. If result-2 is true, evaluate condition-3 as true or false and save it as result-3.
                 If the optional NOT operator precedes condition-3, negate result-3. Evaluate
                 the expression “result-2 AND result-3” and then store the evaluation result in
                 result-2. Return to step 3.
         5.   Evaluate the expression “result-1 operator-1 result-2”. Store the evaluation result
              in result-1.
         6. The final result of the Boolean expression is in result-1.




7831 1784–010                                                                                     5–37
SSG Directives




        Evaluating Boolean Expressions With Parentheses

        When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, parenthetical expressions are allowed. This
        makes the evaluation of expressions much more complex.

             In steps 1, 2c, and 4b of the previous procedure, any of the conditions to be
              evaluated (condition-i) may each be a parenthetical expression. In this case, the
              evaluation of the current Boolean expression is interrupted, and steps 1 through 6
              are performed to evaluate the parenthetical expression as true or false. The NOT
              operator may be used to negate an entire parenthetical expression. Once the final
              value of the parenthetical expression has been determined, the interrupted
              expression evaluation resumes, using the final value as condition-i.
             In steps 2a, 2b, and 4a, the condition-i to be ignored may each be a parenthetical
              expression. In this case, everything after the left parenthesis—including all nested
              parenthetical expressions—is ignored until the matching right parenthesis is
              found.


        Components of Boolean Expressions

        The following is a description of the syntactical components of a Boolean expression:

            condition-i        Any combination of the six simple conditions can be used to
                               provide the condition-i in any order.

            Boolean            Any combination of the AND, OR, and XOR Boolean operators
            operators          can be used in any order. These operators can be supplied by
                               a string, variable reference, or process parameter reference.

                               Note: For clarity, readability, and maintainability, it is
                               recommended that you do not use variable references or
                               process parameter references to supply Boolean operators.

            Left and right     When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, the left and right
            parentheses        parentheses can only be supplied by strings.



        Boolean Operations

             Skipping unnecessary evaluations
              To improve efficiency, SSG does not evaluate the condition to the right of a
              Boolean operator unless it is necessary to determining the result. Skipped
              evaluations are shown in the previous procedure.

                  In steps 2a and 4a, the evaluation of the condition after “false AND” is
                   skipped. Whether the skipped condition is true or false, the result of the
                   evaluation will be false.
                  In step 2b, the evaluation of the condition after “true OR” is skipped. Whether
                   the skipped condition is true or false, the result of the evaluation will be true.




5–38                                                                                  7831 1784–010
                                                                                  SSG Directives




                  In steps 2a, 2b, and 4a, if the condition or parenthetical expression being
                   skipped has format errors, they will not be detected because SSG is searching
                   only for the next AND, OR, or XOR operator.
              Handling NO FIND condition and format errors
                  In steps 1, 2c, and 4b, if a condition-i evaluation results in a NO FIND, the
                   corresponding result-i is set to false. SSG skips the remainder of the condition
                   and searches for the next AND, OR, or XOR operator.
                  If a format error is encountered anytime during the evaluation of the Boolean
                   expression, the final result of the evaluation is false and the remainder of the
                   expression is ignored.
              Tips for debugging Boolean expressions
               When NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, it is possible to have complex expressions
               with many levels of nested parenthetical expressions.

                  When debugging these *IF directives, it may be helpful to set the reserved
                   global variable NOPT$ (see Table 3–9) to a nonzero value immediately before
                   the directive and clear it immediately after the directive. This produces a
                   detailed trace of the Boolean logic as the expression is being evaluated.
                  The OPT file identification N option (see Table 2–4) can also be used to set
                   NOPT$ to a nonzero value at the beginning of skeleton execution. This is
                   likely to produce many more lines of output than setting and clearing NOPT$.
         Searching for a Point to Continue Evaluation

         There are three instances where SSG searches for an AND, OR, or XOR Boolean
         operator as the point to continue evaluation:

              When attempting to satisfy any reference in any simple or compound condition
               results in a NO FIND.
              When a result is false and the next operator is AND (steps 2a and 4a).
              When the result is true and the next operator is OR (step 2b).
         Note: This search will not operate correctly if any part of the condition being
         ignored contains a string, variable reference, or process parameter reference that
         returns a Boolean operator. The search will operate correctly if the Boolean
         operator is supplied by an SGS reference or by a set reference.

         Example 1
         This example uses the following *IF directive and evaluates identically for traditional
         evaluation (NEW_BOOLEAN$ = zero) and new evaluation (NEW_BOOLEAN$ =
         nonzero).

             *IF B IS SET AND VALUE OF A = -1 OR NOT [X,1,1,1] = STOP




7831 1784–010                                                                                  5–39
SSG Directives




        Six operations are performed in the following order:

         1.   Test if variable B is not equal to 0
         2.   Test if variable A = -1 (not evaluated if B = 0)
         3.   Result of (1) AND result of (2)
         4. Test if [X,1,1,1] = STOP (not evaluated if result of (3) is true)
         5.   NOT the result of (4)
         6. Result of (3) OR result of (5) (steps (4) and (5) are ignored if B = 0 and A = -1)

        The result of (6) is the value of the *IF test.

        Example 2
        This example uses the following *IF directive and evaluates identically for traditional
        and new evaluation.

         *IF NOT ELTA HAS COR AND COLUMN SEARCH FROM LAB,6,3,4 FOR ELTA

        Four operations are performed in the following order:

         1.   Test if PCF or TCF contains an element entry with name ELTA
         2.   NOT the result of (1)
         3.   Perform a column search beginning with the sixth SGS with the label LAB,
              field 3, subfield 4 for the image ELTA (not evaluated if ELTA has corrections)
         4. Result of (2) AND result of (3)

        The result of (4) is the value of the *IF test.

        Example 3
        This example illustrates the difference between the traditional and the new
        evaluation priorities.

         *SET TRUE
         *CLEAR FALSE
         *IF +TRUE OR +TRUE AND +FALSE
         *DISPLAY ‘TRUE WHEN NEW_BOOLEAN$ <> 0‘
         *ELSE
         *DISPLAY ‘FALSE WHEN NEW_BOOLEAN$ = 0‘
         *ENDIF

        With traditional evaluation, OR and AND have the same priority so the *IF directive
        is evaluated from left to right. First, ‘+TRUE OR +TRUE’ is evaluated with a result of
        ‘true’. Second, ‘true AND +FALSE’ is evaluated with a final result for the *IF directive
        of ‘false’. The message ‘FALSE WHEN NEW_BOOLEAN$ = 0’ will be displayed in
        the SSG run log.




5–40                                                                                7831 1784–010
                                                                                    SSG Directives




         With new evaluation, AND has a higher priority and is evaluated before OR. First,
         ‘+TRUE AND +FALSE’ is evaluated with a result of ‘false’. Second, ‘+TRUE OR false’
         is evaluated with a final result for the *IF directive of ‘true’. The message ‘TRUE
         WHEN NEW_BOOLEAN$ <> 0’ will be displayed in the SSG run log.

         Example 4
         This example illustrates how, when NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, parentheses can
         be used to change the default order of the evaluation shown in example 3.

          *SET NEW_BOOLEAN$
          *SET TRUE
          *CLEAR FALSE
          *IF (+TRUE OR +TRUE) AND +FALSE
          *DISPLAY ‘TRUE WITHOUT PARENTHESES‘
          *ELSE
          *DISPLAY ‘FALSE WITH PARENTHESES‘
          *ENDIF

         Even though AND has a higher priority than OR, the parenthetical expression will be
         evaluated first. First ‘(+TRUE OR +TRUE)’ is evaluated with a result of ‘true’. Second,
         ‘true AND +FALSE’ is evaluated with a final result for the *IF directive of ‘false’. The
         message ‘FALSE WITH PARENTHESES’ will be displayed in the SSG run log.

         Example 5
         This example illustrates how, when NEW_BOOLEAN$ is nonzero, nested parentheses
         can be used to create complex Boolean expressions.

          *IF NOT((+PERCENT > 50 OR EXCEPTION IS SET) AND ([*DAY] = SAT OR [*DAY] = SUN))

         Proper use of the Boolean operators reduces skeleton processing time since you
         can avoid redundant nesting of *IF directives and duplication of skeleton images.
         (Anticipating NO FIND conditions is another way to reduce processing time.) The
         following example illustrates two functionally equivalent *IF constructions:

          Construction 1                               Construction 2

          *IF [L]                                     *IF [L,1,1,1] = doit
                 *IF [L,1,1,1] = doit                       SKEL to doit
                        SKEL to doit                  *ELSE
                 *ELSE                                      SKEL not to doit
                        SKEL not to doit              *ENDIF
                 *ENDIF
          *ELSE
                 SKEL not to doit
          *ENDIF

         The second construction assumes that if a NO FIND condition exists while evaluating
         the condition “[L,1,1,1] = doit”, the condition is set to false. For a more complicated
         example, suppose an input SGS with label TAPE directs a skeleton to assign (@ASG)
         and write a tape. The file name is in field 1, subfield 1. If field 1, subfield 2 is coded as
         “ASG”, the tape should not be released (@FREE).




7831 1784–010                                                                                     5–41
SSG Directives




        The following constructions are each an attempt to solve this problem:

         Construction 1                                   Construction 2
         (correct, inefficient)                           (correct, efficient)

         *IF [TAPE]                                      *IF [TAPE] AND NOT [TAPE,1,1,2] =
         ASG
             *IF [TAPE,1,1,2]                                #FREE [TAPE,1,1,1].
                    *IF [TAPE,1,1,2] = ASG               *ENDIF
                    *ELSE
                        #FREE [TAPE,1,1,1].
                    *ENDIF
             *ELSE
                    #FREE [TAPE,1,1,1].
             *ENDIF
         *ENDIF

5.2.25.2. Test for Variable Value Conditional
        Format

                   −    IS         
                                    SET
         ∗IF

                                    CLEAR

        Parameter
        variable-name

            A variable name satisfying the description in 3.2.1. Can be supplied by a string or
            by a variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set
            reference that returns a string.

        Description
        You can test either a local or global variable for a nonzero (SET) or a 0 value (CLEAR).
        When you search for variable-name the local variable chain is searched first, from the
        most recently created to the last, and the global variable chain is searched. The first
        variable found with the given name is tested. If the variable does not exist, this is a
        test for existence conditional (see 5.2.25.3) and is false.

        Example 1
          *IF VAR IS SET

        Example 2
          *IF [lab,1,1,1] IS CLEAR




5–42                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                                      SSG Directives




5.2.25.3. Test for Existence Conditional
         Format
             *IF expression

         Parameter
         expression

              Can be a string, variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference,
              set reference, or any other defined conditional whose attempted evaluation could
              produce a NO FIND condition.

         Description
         This test checks to see if the expression exists.

               If a string is supplied, the conditional is always true.
               If a reference is supplied, SSG attempts to satisfy that reference.
                   If evaluation results in a “NO FIND” condition, the conditional is false.
                   If the reference can be satisfied and returns a SYMBOLIC value, the test is
                    true.
                   If the reference returns a NUMERIC value, this is a test for 0 conditional (see
                    5.2.25.4).
               If during evaluation of any conditional a NO FIND condition exists, the conditional
                immediately becomes a test for existence that is always false.
         Note: A test for existence preceded by a NOT negates the test (*IF NOT
         expression).

         Example
             *IF [LAB,1,2,1]

5.2.25.4. Test for Zero Conditional
         Format
             *IF expression

         Parameter
         expression

              Can be a numeric expression, VALUE OF integer expression, or VALUE OF
              numeric expression.

         Description
             If expression has a value of 0, the test is false.
             If expression has a nonzero value, the test is true.




7831 1784–010                                                                                   5–43
SSG Directives




        Example
            *IF +VALID

5.2.25.5. Test for Relation Conditional
        Format
            *IF operand-1 relation operand-2

        Parameters
        operand-n

             Can be supplied by a string, variable reference, process parameter reference,
             SGS reference, set reference, numeric expression, VALUE OF integer expression,
             or VALUE OF numeric expression.

        relation

             Can be <, <=, =, <>, >=, or >.

        Description
        operand-1 is compared to operand-2 according to the relations shown in Table 5–4.


                                Table 5–4. *IF Relations


            Relation                            Description

               <         Tests if operand-1 is less than operand-2
               <=        Tests if operand-1 is less than or equal to operand-2
               =         Tests if operand-1 equals operand-2
               <>        Tests if operand-1 is not equal to operand-2
               >=        Tests if operand-1 is greater than or equal to operand-2
               >         Tests if operand-1 is greater than operand-2


        Types of Relational Tests

        There are two types of relational tests: NUMERIC and SYMBOLIC. Both operands
        are evaluated and all numeric expressions and bracketed references are satisfied.
        Then, the type of operand-1 determines the kind of test as follows:

            If operand-1 is NUMERIC, operand-2, if it is not already NUMERIC, is converted to a
             NUMERIC value (an integer expression, variable name, or number) and a NUMERIC
             comparison is performed. To ensure a NUMERIC comparison, insert a ’+’ before
             operand-1.
            If operand-1 is SYMBOLIC, operand-2 (if NUMERIC), is converted to a SYMBOLIC
             value (edited) and a SYMBOLIC comparison is performed.




5–44                                                                                7831 1784–010
                                                                                 SSG Directives




         SYMBOLIC Comparisons Based on ASCII

         Since SSG operates internally in ASCII, all SYMBOLIC comparisons are based on
         the ASCII collating sequence.

              If special characters (see Table 4–1) are embedded in a symbolic literal operand,
               enclose the operand in pairs of single quotation marks, not double quotation
               marks.

              Bracketed references that resolve to strings containing special characters do not
               need to be surrounded by pairs of single quotation marks.

              A bracketed reference that is surrounded by single quotation marks is not
               resolved and is treated as a symbolic literal operand.

               Note: For SYMBOLIC comparisons, the X option on the @SSG call (see Table
               2–1) does not play a role.

         Example 1
              *IF   A+B   >   C+D

         Example 2
              *IF   [#P]-1 <= [L,A,B,1]

         Example 3
              *IF   [LAB,A-B,C,C+E] = STING

         Example 4
              *IF   [*A_STRING] = ''THIS VALUE''

         Example 5
              *IF +10 < [*NUMBER]

         Example 6
              *CREATE SGS: FILE ''FILE.ELT''
              *IF [FILE,1,1,1] = ''FILE.ELT''

         Example 7
             *SET FILEVAR = 'FILE.ELT'
             *IF [*FILEVAR] = ''FILE.ELT''

SSG Directives




5.2.25.6. Row, Column, or Keyword Search Conditional
        You can view SGSs of a given label as two-dimensional tables. A row in the table
        corresponds to one statement. A column corresponds to field and subfield in all
        statements.

        The following SGSs

         LABL       A,Q    Z,L,P       R6
         LABL       B      S,T         Y
         LABL       D,C

        produce a two-dimensional table like the one shown in Table 5–5.


                                  Table 5–5. Row/Columns Search Table


                                                             Column


                           Field 1,     Field 1,     Field 2,     Field 2,     Field 2,      Field 3,
              Row         Subfield 1   Subfield 2   Subfield 1   Subfield 2   Subfield 3    Subfield 1

       Statement 1            A             Q           Z             L           P            R6
       Statement 2            B                         S             T                         Y
       Statement 3            D             C


        Format
                                    ASC
                 COLUMN  SEARCH ('ASCENDING () FROM ;
                  ROW
          IF
          ∗
                                    DESC
                  KEYWORD
                                    DESCENDING
          label(,start-stmt)(,start-field)(,start-subfield) FOR expression

        Parameters
        ROW, COLUMN, or KEYWORD

              Specifies the type of search to be performed. One of the three types must be
              specified. Can be supplied by a string, process parameter reference, or variable
              reference that returns a symbolic value.

        ASC, ASCENDING, DESC, or DESCENDING

              Optional keywords that specify the direction of the search. ASC and ASCENDING
              specify an ascending search. DESC and DESCENDING specify a descending search.
              Can be supplied by a string, process parameter reference, or variable reference
              that returns a symbolic value.




5–46                                                                                       7831 1784–010
                                                                                SSG Directives




         label

             Can be one of the following:

                An SGS label (see 3.1.1)
                PCFxxxx (affected by the value of PCFNAME$, see 6.4 and 6.7)
                TCFxxxx (affected by the value of TCFNAME$,)
                P (always primary PCF set)
                T (always primary TCF set)
                $SGS (see 3.1.3)
                $PCF (see 6.6)
                $TCF

             Can be supplied by a string or by a variable reference, process parameter
             reference, SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.

         start-stmt

             The statement number (see 3.1.2) with the specified label where the search is
             to begin. Can be supplied by an integer expression or numeric expression.

         start-field

             The field number (see 3.1.2) on the specified start-stmt with the specified label
             where the search is to begin. Can be supplied by an integer expression or numeric
             expression.

         start-subfield

             The subfield number (see 3.1.2) in the specified start-field on the specified start-
             stmt with the specified label where the search is to begin. Can be supplied by an
             integer expression or numeric expression.

         expression

             The image to be searched for. Can be supplied by a string, variable reference,
             process parameter reference, SGS reference, set reference, numeric expression,
             VALUE OF integer expression, or VALUE OF numeric expression. All NUMERIC
             expressions are evaluated. The result is converted to a SYMBOLIC string (edited)
             for the search.




7831 1784–010                                                                                5–47
SSG Directives




        Description
        If the ASC, ASCENDING, DESC, or DESCENDING keywords are omitted, an ascending
        search is assumed. The start-stmt, start-field, and start-subfield can be omitted and,
        if omitted, are assumed to have the value 1.

           Row search

            A row search starts from a particular statement, field, and subfield for a given
            label.

                If an ascending search is specified or assumed, the search proceeds to the
                 next subfield in the current field. If the current subfield is the last in the current
                 field, the search proceeds to the first subfield in the next field.
                If a descending search is specified, the search proceeds to the previous
                 subfield in the current field. If the current subfield is the first in the current
                 field, the search proceeds to the last subfield in the previous field.
                If a descending search is specified, start-field and start-subfield can be
                 arbitrarily large values. In this case, SSG begins the search from the
                 specified start-stmt, the highest field, and the highest subfield.
                The search compares each subfield to the specified expression.
                    If a match is found, the conditional is true and SSG sets the values of the
                     reserved global variables FLD$ and SFLD$ to the field and subfield
                     numbers (see 3.1.2), respectively, where the match is made.
                    If no match is found, the conditional is false and the values of FLD$ and
                     SFLD$ remain unchanged.
           Keyword search

            A keyword search functions the same as a row search, except only the specified
            subfield of each field is compared to the specified expression. For example, if
            subfield 1 is specified on the keyword search, the value of subfield 1 of each field
            on the specified SGS statement is compared to the specified expression.

                If an ascending search is specified or assumed, the search proceeds to the
                 next field.
                If a descending search is specified, the search proceeds to the previous field.
                If a descending search is specified, start-field can be an arbitrarily large value.
                 In this case, SSG begins the search from the specified start-stmt, the highest
                 field, and the specified start-subfield.
                FLD$ and SFLD$ are set the same as in a row search.




5–48                                                                                    7831 1784–010
                                                                                    SSG Directives




               Column search

              A column search starts from a particular statement, field, and subfield for a given
              label.

                   If an ascending search is specified or assumed, the search proceeds to the
                    next statement.
                   If a descending search is specified, the search proceeds to the previous
                    statement.
                   If a descending search is specified, start-stmt can be an arbitrarily large value.
                    In this case, SSG begins the search from the highest statement, the specified
                    start-field, and the specified start-subfield.
                   The search compares each subfield, if it exists, to the specified expression. If
                    the field and subfield do not exist, an ascending search proceeds to the next
                    statement, and a descending search proceeds to the previous statement.
                       As soon as a match is found, the conditional is true and SSG sets the value
                        of the reserved global variable STMT$ to the statement number (see 3.1.2)
                        where the match is made.
                       If no match is found, the conditional is false and the value of STMT$
                        remains unchanged.
         Note: *IF COLUMN (ROW,KEYWORD) SEARCH is more efficient than
         incrementing (*INCREMENT; see 5.2.26) through columns (rows,keywords) and
         performing a test for equality (see 5.2.25.5). *IF [SGS label] is more efficient than
         *IF COLUMN SEARCH FROM $SGS FOR SGS label.

         Example 1
             *IF COLUMN SEARCH FROM [#1],[#2],[#3],[#4] FOR [#6]

         Example 2
             *IF ROW SEARCH FROM LAB,I FOR STRING

         Example 3
             *IF KEYWORD SEARCH FROM LABEL,1,2,I FOR STRING

         Example 4
             *IF COLUMN SEARCH DESCENDING FROM ENTRY,9999999,4,3 FOR [DATE$,1,5,1]




7831 1784–010                                                                                    5–49
SSG Directives




5.2.25.7. Test for Element Entry Conditional
        Format

                            (/   −   ) HAS (ANY) (          ) COR(RECTIONS)
                                                                   PERM
           ∗IF
                          −
                                                                   TEMP



        Parameters
        element-name/version-name

            The name of an element entry satisfying the description in 6.3. Can be supplied by
            a string or by a variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference,
            or set reference that returns a string.

        Description
        You can direct SSG to search for the existence of an element entry. The PCF and TCF
        sets searched depend on how you specify ANY, PERM, and TEMP (see Table 5–6).


                                      Table 5–6. IF PCF TCF Search


                     Specification                                  Search Results

                                       COR        The primary PCF and TCF sets are searched.

                         PERM          COR        The primary PCF set is searched.

                         TEMP          COR        The primary TCF set is searched.

          ANY                          COR        All PCF and TCF sets are searched.

          ANY            PERM          COR        All PCF sets are searched.

          ANY            TEMP          COR        All TCF sets are searched.


        If the element entry is found, the conditional is true. Otherwise, it is false.

        An *element-name/version-name image in an input stream (or the existence of a
        program file element when using the P or T options) causes an element entry to be
        created.

        If an element entry is created with the *CREATE, *CORRECT, or *MERGE directives,
        the *IF test for that entry’s existence is true.

                                                                                SSG Directives




         Example 1
           *IF    [NAME,1,1,1] HAS ANY COR

         Example 2
           *IF    ELT/VER HAS PERM COR

         Example 3
           *IF    NOT ELT/VER HAS ANY TEMP COR

5.2.26. *INCREMENT and *LOOP
         Increment loops can repetitively interpret a block of skeleton images. A local variable
         (see the variable-name parameter) is created and exists for the life of the increment
         loop. The variable name can then be referenced freely in the loop. A local variable
         name can duplicate the name of a global variable. In such an instance, references to
         variable-name result in the local variable value.

         Format

           *INCREMENT variable-name (FROM from-value) (TO to-value) ;

           (BY  − ) (WHILE    −  IS        
                                                 SET


           (   )
                                                 CLEAR

           *LOOP

         Parameters
         variable-name

             Can be supplied by a string satisfying the description in 3.2.1 or by a variable
             reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that
             returns such a string.

         from-value
         to-value
         by-value

             Is an integer expression or numeric expression.

         Description
         An *INCREMENT directive requires a matching *LOOP directive. The images between
         an *INCREMENT directive and its matching *LOOP directive constitute the loop. Each
         execution of an increment loop directs execution (images are interpreted from top to
         bottom). You can nest loops statically, by placing one loop in another, and dynamically
         (*PROCESS) to any level.

         The FROM, TO, BY, or WHILE phrases can be in any order or omitted. When omitted,
         from-value, to-value, and by-value are assumed to be 1, and no WHILE-phrase checks
         are performed.




7831 1784–010                                                                                   5–51
SSG Directives




        The *INCREMENT directive creates a local variable of the name specified and initially
        sets its value equal to from-value (or 1). The first iteration of the increment loop is
        performed if from-value has not exceeded to-value and the WHILE phrase is true (or
        absent). Note that from-value, to-value, and by-value are evaluated only once, on the
        first iteration of the loop. If variables or bracketed references are contained in the
        from-value, to-value, or by-value expressions, changes to the variables or bracketed
        references during loop processing do not affect the tests that are used at the end of
        each iteration to determine if the loop should be terminated.

        After the first execution of the increment loop, the value of variable-name is
        incremented by by-value (or 1). Then, if the new value of variable-name has not
        exceeded to-value and the WHILE phrase is true (or absent), the next iteration takes
        place. This increment test process is repeated after every iteration. When a test fails,
        the increment loop is terminated, image interpretation ends, and the local variable is
        removed. The next image is then the first noncomment skeleton image following the
        *LOOP directive.

        The value of variable-name exceeds to-value if

             ( (present value of variable) - (to-value) ) x (by-value) > 0

        With the above test, you can set the by-value negative. SSG allows for a
        decrementation process. The WHILE phrase tests the specified variable (local or
        global) in that phrase to see if its value is 0 (CLEAR) or nonzero (SET).

        Notes:
             If the name of a global variable is used to supply the variable-name in the WHILE
              phrase, the global variable should not be removed (*REMOVE; see 5.2.35) nor
              should it be changed to a string type variable (*SET, Format 2; see 5.2.39) until
              the loop terminates.
             The local variable should not be changed to a string variable (*SET, Format 2;
              see 5.2.39).


        Example 1
        To repetitively interpret a block of skeleton images until some condition is false:

            *Increment LOOP to very big number
               *If condition
                  DO something
               *ELSE
                  *EXIT LOOP
               *ENDIF
            *Loop

        Evaluating condition may depend on the value of LOOP.




5–52                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                              SSG Directives




         Example 2
         To scan all user defined SGS labels:

          *Increment S from SSGSGS$+1 to [$SGS]
             Handle [$SGS,S,1,1]
          *Loop

         You can scan all PCF and TCF set names by removing the FROM phrase and replacing
         $SGS with $PCF or $TCF, as desired.

         Example 3
         To scan all user defined SGS labels when you might remove (*REMOVE) all SGSs
         with a given label:

          *Increment S from [$SGS] to SSGSGS$+1 by -1
             Handle or remove [$SGS,S,1,1]
          *Loop

         The scan is done backwards to alleviate conflicts with $SGS. See 6.2 for a discussion
         of the effect that removing SGSs has on the SGS label chain. Since the *REMOVE
         directive does not affect what PCF and TCF sets are defined, there is probably no
         need to scan $PCF and $TCF backwards.

         Example 4
         To create an index set:

          *Increment L to [label]
             *If [label,L,...] should be handled
                 *Create SGS: INDEX some function of L and label
             *ENDIF
          *Loop

         Example 5
         To access data through an index set:

          *Increment I to [INDEX]
             Handle [label,[INDEX,I,1,1],...]
          *Loop

         For more information, see 5.2.14.




7831 1784–010                                                                              5–53
SSG Directives




5.2.27. *ITERATE
        The *ITERATE directive allows the next iteration of a loop to take place at any point in
        the loop.

        Format
            *ITERATE (varname)

        Description
        The *ITERATE directive increments the loop control variable. If the TO value has not
        been reached and the WHILE clause is still true, the next iteration of the loop takes
        place.

            If varname is not specified, the iteration takes place on the innermost loop.
            If varname is specified, the loop using the loop control variable specified is
             iterated.
            If varname does not exist, a NO FIND occurs and the directive is ignored.
        Example
            *INCREMENT A TO 50
               *INCREMENT B TO 10
                  *IF +A>30 AND +A<40 AND +B=6
                     *ITERATE A
                  *ENDIF
                  [*A],[*B]
               *LOOP
            *LOOP

5.2.28. *LIST
        The *LIST directive provides skeleton listing control.

        Format
            *LIST

        Description
        The *LIST directive gives you control over the listing of the skeleton. The *LIST
        directive turns listing of the skeleton on in the presence of the E or D options on the
        SSG call. An initial *LIST directive is assumed at the start of each skeleton. No
        directive is required to initially start skeleton listing.

5.2.29. *LOOP( ,E)
        The *LOOP directive signifies the end of an *INCREMENT block (see 5.2.26). The
        E option causes a page eject following the *LOOP.




5–54                                                                                7831 1784–010
                                                                                SSG Directives




5.2.30. *MERGE
         The *MERGE directive merges temporary streams.

         Format
           *MERGE(,options) element-name(/version-name) (TCFxxxx) ;
           (WITH TCFyyyy) (GIVING TCFzzzz)

         Parameters
         options

            Can be a string or a variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS
            reference, or set reference that returns a string. Valid values are

            none     In the absence of a (possibly assumed) GIVING phrase, the result
                     of the correct operation is directed to the generated output
                     stream.
            K        Used in conjunction with the T option or GIVING phrase to
                     additionally direct the images resulting from the merge
                     operation to the generated output stream.
            R        Causes the merge to be a relative merge.
            T        Is ignored if TCF is not a T-option TCF set.
                     If TCFzzzz is not specified, the T option forces SSG to tentatively
                     assume that a GIVING TCFyyyy phrase was specified. It is a
                     tentative assumption because, if TCFyyyy is not a T-option TCF
                     set, the assumption is not made. If the assumption is made, it is
                     the same as if GIVING TCFyyyy was specified on the *MERGE
                     directive.
                     If TCFzzzz (possibly assumed to be TCFyyyy) is a T-option TCF
                     set, the images resulting from the merge are sent to the
                     program file attached to TCFzzzz. A new symbolic element is
                     created (the old one, if it existed, is replaced) with a name equal
                     to the name of the specified element entry (see 6.5)
         element-name/version-name

            Specifies the name of the element entry from which the correction streams are
            to be taken. Can be supplied by a string satisfying the description in 6.3 or by a
            variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference
            that returns such a string.

         TCFxxxx

            Specifies the input temporary stream. If it is omitted, there is no input temporary
            stream. TCFxxxx, if specified, must be a defined TCF set. Can be supplied by a
            string satisfying the description in 6.5 or by a variable reference, process
            parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.




7831 1784–010                                                                               5–55
SSG Directives




        TCFyyyy

           Specifies the input “permanent” stream. (This stream is acted upon as if it were
           a permanent stream from a PCF.) If it is omitted, there is no input “permanent”
           stream. TCFyyyy, if specified, must be a defined TCF set. Can be supplied by
           a string satisfying the description in 6.5 or by a variable reference, process
           parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.

        TCFzzzz

           Specifies the output temporary stream. If it is omitted and if the T option is
           specified on the *MERGE directive and TCFyyyy is a T option TCF set, TCFyyyy is
           assumed; otherwise, the results of the merge are always sent to the generated
           output stream. Can be supplied by a string satisfying the description in 6.5 or by a
           variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference
           that returns such a string.

        Description
        All phrases are optional except element-name/version-name and one TCF set.

        *MERGE directs SSG to merge zero, one, or two TCF streams. In the normal case,
        when two input streams are specified, they are given the names input temporary
        (TCFxxxx) and input “permanent” (TCFyyyy) for two reasons. First, if a conflict occurs,
        corrections from the input “permanent” stream are discarded. In other words,
        temporary overrides “permanent”. Second, when the R option is used, a relative
        merge is performed.

        A relative merge allows the input temporary stream to modify the input “permanent”
        stream. This is similar to the way an input temporary stream modifies an input
        permanent stream when processed with the *CORRECT directive. (Note, however,
        that a *MERGE directive does not require a matching *ENDCORRECT directive.) If a
        specified element entry does not exist, the merge consists of the remaining specified
        entry. If both input streams are omitted, the output resulting from the merge is an
        empty stream. This creates an element in TCFzzzz and attaches an empty stream to it.

        SSG ignores the T option for an element entry that does not exist in TCFzzzz (TCFyyyy
        if assumed). To create a symbolic element in an attached program file for an element
        that does not exist as an element entry, specify a GIVING phrase on the *MERGE,T
        directive. In the absence of the GIVING phrase, the T option is ignored. Normally, an
        element in an attached program file is revised and reinserted into the program file,
        simulating an update process. In either case, place a GIVING phrase on the *MERGE,T
        directive to ensure modification of the attached program file.




5–56                                                                             7831 1784–010
                                                                               SSG Directives




         If you do not specify the R option, SSG combines the two input streams. Line change
         statements in both streams are based on a PCF stream and base level symbolic
         element. (The result of this merge will be used to revise that PCF stream.) The
         corrections are put in sequential order. (Each correction stream must be in sequential
         order before the merge.) In this type of merge, TCFxxxx does not make relative
         corrections to TCFyyyy. All relative corrections in both streams are based on a PCF
         stream. Sequence errors are flagged and the associated correction images are
         discarded. In the case of conflicts, the input temporary stream takes precedence over
         the input “permanent” stream. You can use the reserved, global variable CONFLC$
         (see Table 3–9) to monitor this.

         If you specify the R option on the *MERGE directive, SSG assumes that the input
         “permanent” stream has already been merged with a PCF stream, producing a revised
         PCF stream. SSG also assumes relative corrections in the input temporary stream are
         relative to the revised PCF stream. SSG performs a merge between the input
         temporary stream and the revised PCF stream, producing a new revised PCF stream.
         The output of the merge is the TCF stream needed to revise the original PCF stream.
         Sequence errors are flagged and the associated correction images are discarded. In
         the case of conflicts, the input temporary stream takes precedence over the input
         permanent stream. You can use the reserved, global variable CONFLC$
         (see Table 3–9) to monitor this. This is a powerful merge with interesting applications.
         For an application of *MERGE,R, refer to Appendix B.

         Note: When you are resolving line number conflicts, the WITH phrase of *MERGE
         specifies the input temporary stream that has the same priority level as if it were a
         permanent stream.In addition, where *CORRECT assumes PCF and a matching
         TCF set, *MERGE makes no such assumption. Use of the *CORRECT,P and
         *MERGE directive is analogous. Compatibility with previous levels of SSG is
         retained when logic dictates.

         Restrictions
         Several restrictions apply when using *MERGE because of conflicts that occur and
         erroneous merges that are accepted. See 5.2.7 for *CORRECT-related restrictions.

            -N-conflicts with -N when merging corrections. For example, an input temporary
             stream that looks like this
                *element-name/version-name
                -577-
                -577

             produces the following diagnostic messages:

                ERROR IN PROCESSING ELEMENT element-name/version-name
                SEQUENCE OR FORMAT ERROR ON FOLLOWING IMAGE -> ;
                POSSIBLE MERGE INTERNAL CONFLICT
                -577

             The preceding diagnostic indicates an error because the -577- and -577 images are
             in the same temporary stream. The last image, -577, is displayed and does not
             appear in the output temporary stream.




7831 1784–010                                                                               5–57
SSG Directives




            If the images are each in different temporary streams, a merge produces a
            conflict, not an error. A conflict produces the following diagnostic messages:

              CONFLICT IN RELATIVE CORRECTIONS: *MERGE facsimile
              -577(-)

            The image listed in the message, either -577 or -577-, is derived from the input
            permanent stream and does not appear in the output temporary stream.

           Conflicts—The following correction lines result in a conflict:
              -n,m/0,0/n-k,new-m
              -greater-than-new-m-but-less-than-m,does-not-matter

            For example, the following construction would not work:

              -10,20/0,0/5,15
              -17,18

            In this example, -17,18 conflicts with -10,20 and, due to the nature of a merge,
            is treated as an error. However, *CORRECT can correctly handle this TCF stream.
            The following construction can circumvent the problem:

              -10,20/0,0/5,15
              -10,20/n/17,18

            In this example, n is the number of data images following -10,20.

           An erroneous merge may be accepted given the following two TCF streams
              A)    *ELT                  B)   *ELT
                    -2,2/0,0/3,3               -2,2/0,0/3,3
                    -2/0,0/1,1                 *ELT
                                                 -2/0,0/1,1

            and given the two possibilities for *MERGE

              1)   *MERGE                 2)   *MERGE,R

            The only combination that correctly issues a sequence error message is B2.
            The others produce this construction:

              *ELT
              -2,2/0,0/3,3
              -2/0,0/1,1

            This TCF stream is erroneous and always rejected by *CORRECT.

           *MERGE,R restrictions—The possible temporary streams shown in Table 5–7,
            input to MERGESKEL in SYSKEL utility file, all produce erroneous results. These
            constructs are not often used. If you encounter the incorrectly generated result,
            correct the runstream as indicated before continuing.

                *ELT       *ELT        *ELT
                -1/4/5     -1/4/5      -1/4/5
                TLine1     TLine1      TLine1
                *ELT       -1/-1/6     -1/4/6
                -5/1/6
                *ELT       *ELT        *ELT

                -1/4/5     -1/4/5      -1/4/5
