5.2.31. *MESSAGE
        Format
          *MESSAGE(,options) text

        Parameters
        options

           Can be supplied by any combination of characters and, if LBRK$ is clear, bracketed
           references resolving to a string (see Table 5–8).


                                 Table 5–8. MESSAGE Options


            Option                                    Description

                  E     Increments the value of ERRCNT$ by 1.

                  I     Displays this message in the SSG run log.
                        *****MESSAGE (,options) text

                  N     Prevents the display of the message in the SSG run log.

                  L     Generates an ER ACSF$ of the following image:
                        @LOG SSG*MESSAGE (,options) text

                  O     Displays the message upon the operator console. The T option is assumed
                        when the O option is specified.

                  T     Affects both contents of the message displayed in the SSG run log and the
                        text of the @LOG image submitted to ACSF$. The image used consists
                        solely of the text portion of the message, where text refers to those
                        characters following the first space character after options. Provides for
                        exclusively user-formatted messages.

                  X     Increments the value of ERRCNT$ by one. Aborts the run via ER ERR$. An
                        ER ERR$ is not performed if the run is a demand run, the run is not in
                        @BKRPT mode and the last image in the runstream read by SSG was not
                        an @ADD image. In any case, SSG always unconditionally terminates
                        processing.




5–60                                                                               7831 1784–010
                                                                                    SSG Directives




         text

                Can be supplied by any combination of characters and if LBRK$ is clear, by
                bracketed references. The text of the message, including (,options), is truncated
                at 120 characters. SSG processes text as if it is a quoted string, even though
                quotation marks are not required and should not be specified. Continuation is not
                allowed anywhere in the *MESSAGE directive, except within bracketed
                references, if LBRK$ is clear. If a semicolon is included in the image it is processed
                as part of the text. If a semicolon is included and if the L option is specified, an ER
                (error type 04) error code 040 contingency occurs.

         Description
         Processing a *MESSAGE directive is transparent. You can place this directive
         anywhere in the skeleton except between an image and its continuation image.

         In the absence of the N or I options, SSG uses the value of IOPT$ to control message
         display in the SSG run log. In other words, the message is displayed if IOPT$ has a
         nonzero value.

         Note: You can also use the I and N options to override (not change) the current
         value of IOPT$.

         After the message is or is not displayed, the L option is processed. Then the E and X
         options are processed. If the E and X options are specified together on the same
         *MESSAGE directive, the value of ERRCNT$ is incremented once by 1.

         Note: Using the *MESSAGE directive to display a message in the SSG run log is
         much more efficient than using *CREATE to create SGSs.

         Example 1
           *MESSAGE, IT        *WARNING*     [#1] is ignored because ...

         Example 2
           *MESSAGE, IET         *ERROR* ...

         Example 3
           *MESSAGE, XT        *FATAL ERROR* ...

         Example 4
           *MESSAGE, NO        WRITE RING IN 532 PLEASE




7831 1784–010                                                                                     5–61
SSG Directives




5.2.32. *MULTIPLY
        The *MULTIPLY directive performs variable multiplication.

        Format
          *MULTIPLY number BY number GIVING variable-name

        Parameters
        number

            Is an integer expression or numeric expression.

        variable-name

            Can be supplied by a string satisfying the description in 3.2.1 or by a variable
            reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that
            returns such a string.

        Description
        The product of the first number multiplied by the second number is the value of the
        given variable-name. If no local variable with that name exists, a global variable is
        searched for. If no global variable with that name exists, one is created. Once
        created, this global variable exists until it is removed (*REMOVE; see 5.2.35).

        Note: If evaluation of either number results in an arithmetic overflow or if the
        multiplication produces an arithmetic overflow, an error diagnostic is displayed in
        the SSG run log and the largest or smallest appropriate representable value is used
        as the result.

        Example 1
          *MULTIPLY       A+B BY C-D GIVING E

        Example 2
          *MULTIPLY       [#A]+[B,1,C+D,2] BY 7 GIVING [#P]

5.2.33. *PCONTROL
        The *PCONTROL directive allows the SSG user to perform an ER APRTCN$.

        Format
          *PCONTROL ‘APRTCN$ image‘

        Description
        After all bracketed references have been satisfied, use the image enclosed in
        quotation marks as the print control image for an ER APRTCN$. To obtain a single
        quotation mark in the print control image, use pairs of single quotation marks.




5–62                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                                 SSG Directives




         Example 1
           *PCONTROL     ‘d,@@ESC O‘

         Example 2
           *PCONTROL     ‘H,,,[Heading,1,1,1,SGS_STRING$,99,99] ‘

         Example 3
           *PCONTROL     ‘H,,,that‘‘s the last one‘

5.2.34. *PROCESS
         Format 1
           *PROCESS(,option) define-name (proc-para-1 proc-para-2 ...)

         Format 2
           *define-name (proc-para-1 proc-para-2 ...)

         Parameters
         option

            A     Ignores error if NO FIND
                  occurs.

         define-name

             For format 1, this parameter can be supplied by a string satisfying the description
             in 5.2.10 or by a variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference,
             or set reference that returns such a string. For format 2, no references are
             allowed. SKEL$ reinvokes the control section of the skeleton.

         proc-para

             Can be supplied by a string, variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS
             reference, set reference, or numeric expression. If NEW_PROCESS$ is set to 0, a
             single asterisk ( * ) followed by a bracketed reference directs the evaluation of the
             string that satisfies an indirect reference.

         Description
         The *PROCESS directive directs SSG to retrieve and process a define packet and
         passes process parameters to that packet.

         If an asterisk ( * ) is found in column 1, and the directive following it is not an SSG
         directive, a *PROCESS directive is assumed. This allows for calls of define packets.
         Thus, *XYZ is the same as *PROCESS XYZ. (The define-name for the assumed
         *PROCESS directive call cannot contain process parameter, SGS, set, or variable
         references.)




7831 1784–010                                                                                  5–63
SSG Directives




        If NEW_PROCESS$ is set to zero, SSG examines all the parameters specified on the
        *PROCESS directive, evaluating all numeric expressions and references and converting
        any resulting numeric values to their symbolic representations. Usually,
        only SYMBOLIC parameters are passed to a define packet. If an SGS reference or set
        reference supplying a process parameter results in a NO FIND condition, and the A
        option is specified on the @SSG call, a NO FIND process parameter is created. A NO
        FIND process parameter results in a NO FIND condition when referenced. However, a
        test for existence conditional can be used to test for such a process parameter.

        If NEW_PROCESS$ is nonzero, SSG satisfies all bracketed references, and then
        creates an SGS with a label of # that may be referenced in the define packet like
        an SGS. However, this # SGS cannot be referenced by using the $SGS reserved
        label.For information on referencing *PROCESS parameters within a define packet,
        refer to 3.3.

        Example 1
          *PROCESS    NAME

        Example 2
          *DRAW_SCALE

        Example 3
          *PROCESS    [NAME,1,1,1]

        Example 4
          *PROCESS    MULTIPLY   A+B   C-D   E+F+G

        Example 5
          *PROCESS    VERY_LONG_DESCRIPTIVE_NAME

        Example 6
          *SET_MARGINS       88,8,4




5–64                                                                           7831 1784–010
                                                                                SSG Directives




5.2.35. *REMOVE
         The *REMOVE directive performs dynamic contraction of internal chains.

         Format 1
           *REMOVE SGS sgs-label(,sgs-start-stmt(,number-to-remove))

         Format 2

                                       (/   −   ) (FROM  −   )
                         PERM
           ∗REMOVE
                                          −
                         TEMP

         Format 3
           *REMOVE VARIABLE variable-name

         Parameters
         sgs-label

            Specifies the SGSs to be removed. Can be supplied by a string satisfying the
            description in 3.1.1 or by a variable reference, process parameter reference, SGS
            reference, or set reference that returns such a string.

         sgs-start-stmt

            Specifies the statement number with the given sgs-label where the removal
            will start. Can be an integer expression or a numeric expression. This number
            is optional (1 is assumed if omitted).

         number-to-remove

            Specifies the number of SGSs to remove. Can be an integer expression or
            a numeric expression. This number is optional (1 is assumed if omitted);
            number-to-remove can evaluate to zero.

         element-name/version-name

            Specifies the element entry to be removed from set-name. Can be supplied by a
            string satisfying the description in 6.3 or by a variable reference, process
            parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.

         set-name

            Specifies the PCF (PERM) or TCF (TEMP) set from which the specified element
            entry is to be removed. Can be supplied by a string satisfying the descriptions in
            6.4 (PERM) or 6.5 (TEMP) or by a variable reference, process parameter reference,
            SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.




7831 1784–010                                                                               5–65
SSG Directives




        variable-name

             Specifies the global variable to be removed. Can be supplied by a string satisfying
             the description in 3.2.1 or by a variable reference, process parameter reference,
             SGS reference, or set reference that returns such a string.

        Description
        The *REMOVE directive performs three functions.

            Removes a specified number of SGSs with the specified SGS label starting at the
             first or specified SGS statement.
             If there are no SGSs within the range specified, no SGS are removed. If there are
             no SGSs with label sgs-label left before or after the attempted removal, sgs-label
             is removed from the SGS label chain, which can affect references using $SGS.
             This means if sgs-label occupied the i th slot in the SGS label chain ([$SGS,i,1,1]),
             it no longer does; and [$SGS,i,1,1] either results in a NO FIND condition or returns
             the SGS label that occupied slot i+1; [$SGS,i,1,1] becomes what was [$SGS,i+1,1,1].

            Removes the element entry with the specified name from the PERM chain (the
             primary PCF set is assumed in the absence of set-name) or TEMP chain (the
             primary TCF set is assumed in the absence of set-name).
             Once removed, the element entry and any attached correction stream are
             destroyed during the SSG execution. This change is internal to SSG. The files used
             for input are not changed. If set-name is supplied, it must match the PERM or
             TEMP keyword supplied. If the element entry does not exist, the *REMOVE
             directive is ignored. The PCF or TCF set-name is not removed from set-specified,
             the PCF or TCF set chain, even if there are zero element entries left in set-name
             before or after removal.

            Removes variable-name from the global variable chain.
             If variable-name does not exist in the global variable chain, the *REMOVE directive
             is ignored. If a reserved global variable is removed, SSG retains the last value
             associated with it, even if it is redefined (see use of the $ character in 3.2.3). A
             global variable specified on the WHILE phrase of an *INCREMENT directive should
             not be removed while processing that increment loop.

        The *REMOVE directive makes available for reuse parts of the D-bank of the SSG
        processor that were used to store data no longer needed.

        Example 1
        To remove all SGSs with a given label:

            *REMOVE SGS label,1,[label]

        This also removes label from the SGS label chain.




5–66                                                                                7831 1784–010
                                                                                  SSG Directives




         Example 2
           *CREATE SGS: NAME ELT/VER
           *REMOVE [NAME,1,1,1]

         Example 3
           *REMOVE      ELT/VER FROM TCFXYZ

5.2.36. *RESTORE
         The *RESTORE directive provides a method of restoring an SGS environment that
         was previously saved via the *SAVE directive.

         Format
           *RESTORE(,option) SGS FROM file.(element)

         Parameters
         option

            A      Do not terminate in error if the source does not exist or if SSG did not
                   create the source.

         file.(element)

                Restore the SGS environment from the file with this name.

         Description
         The *RESTORE directive provides a method of establishing a previously saved
         environment in SSG. The keywords SGS and FROM are required before the file
         specification. The file specification must be in system standard file name notation.
         The information in the defined source must have been created by SSG. If the A option
         is not specified, SSG terminates if the specified file or element does not exist or was
         not created by SSG. Any number of SGS sets can be restored in a given skeleton.

         As the SGSs are restored, they are always added to the end of the chain of SGSs with
         the same label in the same order in which they were saved. The SGSs are saved and
         restored in SSG internal format; they are never printed in the SSG run log.

         Note: With the *RESTORE directive, the values of the reserved global variables
         SOPT$ and IOPT$ are ignored.




7831 1784–010                                                                                 5–67
SSG Directives




5.2.37. *RETURN
        The *RETURN directive allows early termination of a define packet or the main
        skeleton.

        Format
          *RETURN (STOP)

        Description
        If *RETURN is encountered in a define packet, control is returned to the statement
        following the define packet call (*PROCESS). If *RETURN or *RETURN STOP is
        encountered in the main skeleton or if *RETURN STOP is encountered in a define
        packet, normal skeleton termination takes place. All remaining directives are skipped
        and the generated output stream is handled as described in 2.4.

        Example
          *IF error IS SET
             *RETURN
          *ENDIF

        If the variable ERROR is nonzero, the skeleton execution is stopped and normal exit
        is taken.

5.2.38. *SAVE
        The *SAVE directive provides a method of saving the SGS environment.

        Format
          *SAVE SGS (LABEL,label) IN name

        Parameters
        label
            Is an SGS label; only the SGSs with this label will be saved. If no SGSs for
            the specified label exist, processing will continue with the next directive line.

        name
            Can be a file or an element, and must be in standard filename.eltname notation.
            Use any combination of characters. If LBRK$ is clear, use bracketed references.
            If the file does not exist, SSG creates a temporary file. If a program-file element
            already exists, then the existing element will be marked for deletion.

        Description
        The *SAVE directive saves the user-defined SGS environment or the SGSs with the
        indicated label in the location specified on the directive line. The information is saved
        in omnibus format. None of the SGSs defined by SSG are saved by the *SAVE
        directive. The LABEL keyword must be specified if a one-label save is desired;
        otherwise, the entire user-defined SGS environment will be saved.




5–68                                                                                7831 1784–010
                                                                                SSG Directives




5.2.39. *SET
         The *SET directive creates and changes variable values.

         Format 1
          *SET variable-name (TO number)

         Format 2
          *SET variable-name (TO ‘string’)

         Parameters
         variable-name

             Can be supplied by a string satisfying the description in 3.2.1 or by a variable
             reference, process parameter reference, SGS reference, or set reference that
             returns such a string.

         number

             Is an arithmetic expression (see Table 4–1). An undefined variable within an
             expression has a value of 0. If the expression contains a bracketed reference
             to an undefined variable, then a NO FIND error is generated.

         ’string’

             Can be any combination of characters. If LBRK$ is clear, all bracketed references
             will be satisfied. To obtain a single quotation mark in the string, two single
             quotation marks should be used.

         Description
         An equal sign (=) can be used instead of the keyword TO. If the TO or = phrase is
         omitted, number is assumed to be 1.

         The value of the last local variable with the given name is set to number. If no local
         variable with the given name exists, a global variable with that name is searched for
         and set to number. If no global variable with that name exists, one is created and given
         the value number. Once created, this global variable exists until it is removed
         (*REMOVE).

         Use the *SET directive to create or change the value of a numeric or string variable.
         To allow the use of nonprintable ASCII characters in string variables, the construct
         C ‘octal-number’ is also allowed. The number is left justified and zero-filled to the
         nearest quarter word (9 bits).




7831 1784–010                                                                                   5–69
SSG Directives




        Example
          *SET ESC = C’033’
          *SET CLEAR_SCREEN = ’[*ESC]e[*ESC]M’

        This example results in the variable CLEAR_SCREEN having the following internal
        ASCII value:

          033145033115

        That value can then be printed by the following entry:

          *DISPLAY clear_screen

        You can also use the *SET directive to make numeric computations. The arithmetic
        operators +, -, /, and * are recognized in expressions; spaces between operands and
        operators are ignored. The expression is evaluated from left to right, with
        multiplication and division performed before addition and subtraction. In order to allow
        for hexadecimal, octal, and binary arithmetic, the constructs H’hex-number’, O’octal-
        number ’ and B’binary-number ’ are allowed.

        Note: If evaluation of number results in an arithmetic overflow, an error diagnostic
        is displayed in the SSG run log and the largest or smallest representable value, as
        appropriate, is used.

        The *SET directive is scanned twice with all bracketed references being satisfied
        on the first scan. You can use it to substitute an equation, used several times, for
        a *SET directive.

        Example 1
          *SET EQUATION = ‘3*Radius*Radius‘
          *SET AREA1 = [*EQUATION] + 10
                .
                .
                .
          *SET AREA6 = [*EQUATION] + 13

        Example 2
          *SET   VAR

        Example 3
          *SET   VAR TO A+B-C *3 +2

        Example 4
          *SET   [VAR,I,1,1]

        Example 5
          *SET   [#P] to [VAR,I,1,1]+VAR

        Example 6
          SET VALUE = 23 * O’17’ + H’F’ / B’101’



5–70                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                                            SSG Directives




         Example 7
           *SET STRING = ’This is a string variable’

         Example 8
           *SET STRING_TWO TO ’ [*STRING], it is very nice’

         Example 9
           *SET NUMBER = H’E325’ * H’1A’ - O’52’

         Note: Be careful when putting a string variable reference within a string variable
         set since a single quotation mark anywhere in the variable reference terminates
         scanning.
         Example 10
           *SET ST1 = 'Where''s the fire'
           *SET ST2 = '[*ST1] truck? '

         This example sets the value of ST1 to

           Where's the fire

         and the value of ST2 to

           Where

         Example 11
           *SET ST1 = 'Where''''s the fire'
           *SET ST2 = '[*ST1] truck? '

         This example sets the value of ST1 to

           Where''s the fire

         and the value of ST2 to

           Where's the fire truck?

5.2.40. *SORT
        The *SORT directive allows dynamic sorting of SGSs, PCFs, and TCFs.

        Format

            SORT(,   ) TCF       )
                             SGS
        ∗                           (
                             PCF

        Parameters
        options

              Can be supplied by any combination of characters and, if LBRK$ is clear, bracketed
              references are resolved.

              A   Do not terminate in error if the item to sort does not exist.
              D   Sort the values in descending order.
              L   Sort across all fields.
              X   Sort numerically. The subfield values must be numeric characters.
                  Note: This numeric sort is different from the alphanumeric sort
                  performed with the @SSG call line X option shown in Table 2–1.


        name

              For sorting TCF or PCF sets, the set name is used. For sorting SGSs, the following
              format of the NAME specification is used:

                  (label(,start-sgs(,start-field(,start-subfield))))

        If start-sgs, start-field, or start-subfield are omitted, the default value of 1 is used.
        If the name parameter is omitted, then all labels or sets are sorted.

        Description
        The *SORT directive allows dynamic sorting of SGSs and PCF/TCF set entries.
        Sorting is done in ascending order, unless otherwise requested by the D
        (descending) option.

             For PCF/TCF sets, sorting is always done on field 3 (the concatenated
              element/version name). Duplicates are not removed as they are with the
              Q or O options.
             For SGSs, start-sgs is the first statement number to sort. All statements
              following start-sgs will be sorted; start-field and start-subfield indicate the
              subfield position across which the sorting will take place.




5–72                                                                                 7831 1784–010
                                                                                   SSG Directives




             If the L option is specified and a tie occurs while sorting, the following fields
              are used to determine the proper order. If a field or subfield does not exist,
              it is taken to be a low value.
              Note: The L option does not sort PCF/TCF sets. This option has no meaning
              if the X option is specified.

         Following a *SORT directive, the *ACCEPT, *ADD, *CREATE, and *RESTORE directives
         can be used to add new SGSs to the sorted chain of SGSs with the same label, with
         these constraints:

             Once you add new SGSs, the chain may not be in the same order as before.
              If possible, wait until all the new SGSs have been added before using the *SORT
              directive.
             When the global variable SOPT$ is nonzero, the order in which SGSs are added
              to the chain of SGSs with the same label is unpredictable under these conditions:
                 The SGS label has been sorted with the D option specified.
                 The SGS label has been sorted with a start-field and/or start-subfield value
                  other than 1.
         For more details, refer to 3.2.3.3.

         Example 1
             *SORT SGS TEST

         Example 2
             *SORT,L SGS Elements

         Example 3
             *SORT,D TCF

         Example 4
             *SORT,DL SGS

         Example 5
             *SORT PCF SET1

         Example 6
             *SORT,X SGS VALUES,1,3,2

SSG Directives




5.2.41. *UNLIST
        The *UNLIST directive provides skeleton listing control.

        Format
          *UNLIST

        Description
        The *UNLIST directive gives the SSG programmer control over the listing of the
        skeleton. The *UNLIST directive turns off the listing of the skeleton following the
        *UNLIST directive. (Turn the listing on again with the *LIST directive.)

        Note: This directive works only with the E or D options specified on the SSG call
        line.

5.2.42. *WAIT
        The *WAIT directive allows delays to occur in skeleton processing.

        Format
          *WAIT arithmetic-expression

        Description
        Use the *WAIT directive to delay skeleton execution. You may want to do this
        when printing messages intended to be read on a demand terminal or when skeleton
        interpretation needs to be slowed down.

        The arithmetic expression can include the * and / operators. (For acceptable arithmetic
        expressions see Table 4–1.) The value obtained by evaluating the arithmetic
        expression is the number of seconds to wait.

        An undefined variable within an expression has a value of 0. If the expression contains
        a bracketed reference to an undefined variable, then a NO FIND error is generated.
        A maximum of 86,400 seconds (24 hours) is allowed.

        Example 1
          *WAIT 30

        Example 2
          *WAIT TIME_DELAY

        Example 3
          *WAIT 30 * WAIT_VALUE - 12




5–74                                                                              7831 1784–010
Section 6
Permanent and Temporary Streams

         This section describes the permanent correction file (PCF) and the temporary
         correction file (TCF). It describes access and references to the files, line changes, and
         the revised temporary correction (RTC) stream.


6.1. Introduction
         The Executive Control Language (ECL) applies a group of correction images to a
         symbolic element, producing a new, possibly virtual symbolic element. To see the
         correction image formats, refer to the Executive Control Language (ECL)
         and FURPUR Reference Manual (7830 7949).

         Example
             @MASM,U filename.element-name...

             (correction images)

             @EOF


6.2. PCF and TCF Sets
         You often need a skeleton to generate a symbolic stream (generated output stream)
         that contains correction images, as shown in the example in 6.1. To make program
         corrections separately from the skeleton, you can group correction streams into PCF
         and TCF sets (see Appendix B).

         A PCF set maintains groups of changes (element entries) to some base level symbolic
         elements.

         A TCF set modifies or adds changes to an existing PCF entry. A TCF entry can contain
         two types of line-change statements:

             Insertion—those relative to the base level symbolic elements being modified.
             Modification—those relative to an already existing line-change statement in the
              PCF. Both statements can be mixed in a TCF stream.




7831 1784–010                                                                                   6–1
Permanent and Temporary Streams




       Each PCF and TCF set can contain unlimited element entries. An element entry can
       have a group of symbolic images attached. This is generally referred to as a correction
       stream. Specifically, correction streams (element entries) in PCF sets are referred to
       as permanent correction streams (PCF streams). Those in TCF sets are referred to as
       temporary correction streams (TCF streams).

       You can use skeleton directives to merge a permanent stream and a temporary
       stream (*CORRECT) or two temporary streams (*MERGE). You can direct the result of
       a merge to the generated output stream or a program file, or both.

       Note: Sequence errors in the input permanent stream are not flagged. Always
       using SSG to maintain the PCF does not cause problems because the input
       permanent stream is derived from old input temporary streams that are checked for
       sequence errors.


6.3. Element Entries
       An image containing an asterisk ( * ) in the first character position, column 1,
       immediately followed by an element-name/version-name defines an element entry.
       All images that follow such an *element-name/version-name image are attached to
       that element entry until the next entry is defined. The element-name and version-
       name can each contain up to 12 characters; alphabetic, numeric, -, or $. The /version-
       name is optional.

       The format of the entry is as follows:

         *element-name/version-name [subfield 1-1,subfield 1-n...subfield 2-1, ;

         subfield 2-n...subfield n-n...]

         (corrections associated with element-name/version-name)

       The element-name and version-name are not case sensitive. That is, ABC/DEF is the
       same as aBc/DeF.

       Enter element entries after you define the PCF or TCF set to which they will belong.
       Attach the name of the element entered (element-name/version-name) to the
       selected PCF or TCF set, and the correction stream to the element entry. An SGS is
       created, with the set name as the label, element/version as field 1, and any fields
       specified on the element entry as fields 2 through n. An attached element entry is said
       to be “in” a PCF or TCF set. A PCF or TCF set can contain any number of element
       entries. You can flag duplicate element entries by specifying the O and Q options on
       the @SSG call (see Table 2–1).




6–2                                                                             7831 1784–010
                                                        Permanent and Temporary Streams




6.4. Permanent Streams (PCF)
         You can define many PCF sets, each with a maximum seven-character, case-
         independent set-name, PCFxxxx.

         Each PCF set is a collection of element entries. Images defining element entries can
         come from any combination of system data format (SDF) files or symbolic elements
         specified on the @SSG call on a PCFxxxx file-id statement, or as images in the
         runstream following a PCFxxxx file-id statement.

         There are two other means of defining a PCF set. You can specify the P option
         (see Table 2–1) in conjunction with a program file (param-7, see 2.1.2) on the @SSG
         call or use the P option (see Table 2–2) in conjunction with a program file on a PCFxxxx
         file-id statement. With this type of input, a program file is attached to the selected PCF
         set, and the name and contents of each element entry are taken from each
         nondeleted symbolic element in the attached program file. You can define a PCF set
         using SDF input. Later you can make a P option PCF set by using a PCFxxxx,P file-id
         statement. Similarly, you can define a P option PCF set and later extend it using SDF
         input from PCFxxxx file-id statements. You can attach one program file to a given PCF
         set. Also, since each element entry is taking its name and content from the symbolic
         element in a program file, you should not use an *element-name/version-name image
         at the start of the symbolic images.

         Another way of defining a PCF set is through the GIVING phrase on the *CORRECT
         directive.

         The primary PCF set, $PCF, is always defined, even when empty (see 6.6).

         You can list the input or output PCF sets, or both, using the F and/or J/R options on the
         @SSG call. You will see the following message after the stream images in the PCF set
         are listed:

             ******** PCFxxxxSTREAM TOTAL IMAGES:n ********

         where:

         PCFxxxx

               is the name of the PCF set listed.

         n
               is the total number of change images for the elements listed. The count does not
               reflect images for elements not listed. With the use of the J option, all elements
               cannot be listed.




7831 1784–010                                                                                  6–3
Permanent and Temporary Streams




6.5. Temporary Streams (TCF)
       You define TCF sets with a maximum seven-character, not case sensitive, set-name,
       TCFxxxx.

       Each TCF set is a collection of element entries. Any number of element entries can be
       in a TCF set. Images defining element entries can come from any combination of SDF
       files or symbolic elements specified on the @SSG call, on a TCFxxxx file-id statement,
       or as images in the runstream following a TCFxxxx file-id statement.

       You can also define a TCF set by specifying the T option (see Table 2–1) in conjunction
       with a program file (param-7, see 2.1.2) on the @SSG call or by using the T option (see
       Table 2–3) in conjunction with a program file on a TCFxxxx file-id statement. With this
       type of input, a program file is attached to the selected TCF set and the name and
       contents of each element entry are taken from each nondeleted symbolic element in
       the attached program file.

       You can define a TCF set that uses SDF input. Later you can make it into a T option
       TCF set by using a TCFxxxx,T file-id statement. Similarly, you can define a T option
       TCF set and later extend it using SDF input from TCFxxxx file-id statements. One
       program file can be attached to a given TCF set. Also since the element entries get
       their names and contents from the symbolic elements of a program file, you should
       not use an *element-name/version-name image at the start of the symbolic
       corrections.

       Another way of defining a TCF set is through the GIVING phrase on the *MERGE
       directive.

       The primary TCF set, $TCF, is always defined, even when empty (see 6.6).

       You can list the input TCF set, output TCF set, or both, by using the G or U option on
       the @SSG call. You will see the following message after the stream images in the TCF
       set are listed:

           ******** TCFxxxxSTREAM TOTAL IMAGES:n ********

       where:

       TCFxxxx

             is the name of the TCF set listed.

       n

             is the total number of correction images for the elements listed.




6–4                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                        Permanent and Temporary Streams




6.6. PCF and TCF Set Access
         Two reserved labels, $PCF and $TCF, are defined. They function in a manner similar
         to $SGS and SGS labels. [$PCF,i,1,1] returns the name of the i th-defined PCF set,
         and [$TCF,i,1,1] returns the name of the i th-defined TCF set. [$PCF] and [$TCF] return
         the number of PCF and TCF sets defined. [$PCF,1,1,1] and [$TCF,1,1,1] are always
         PCF and TCF. In other words, the primary PCF and TCF sets are always defined.

         PCF and TCF sets are defined in the order input. SSG reads across the @SSG call and
         then down through file-id statements.


6.7. Set References
         You can use a special mechanism, similar to the SGS referencing mechanism
         described in 3.1.2, to access element entries. For each PCF and TCF set you define, use
         set-name, PCFxxxx or TCFxxxx, to access the entries within the set.

         Since a set-name is attached to the PCF or TCF entry and to an SGS, two reserved
         global variables, PCFNAME$ and TCFNAME$, are defined.Initially, PCFNAME$ has a
         value of 0, and TCFNAME$ has a value of 1.

            If PCFNAME$ (TCFNAME$) is set to a nonzero value, SSG attempts to satisfy a
             bracketed reference that looks like a PCF (TCF) set reference by searching the PCF
             set (TCF set) chain.
                If the set-name is found, the reference is assumed to pertain to a valid PCF
                 (TCF) set reference as described in Table 6–1.
                If the set-name is not found, SSG assumes the reference is an SGS reference
                 and the SGS label chain is searched.
            If PCFNAME$ (TCFNAME$) has a value of 0, the reference is assumed to be an
             SGS reference and the PCF set (TCF set) chain is not searched for a matching set-
             name.

         The primary PCF set has a synonym, P, for referencing. A reference with label P refers
         to PCF, independent of the value of PCFNAME$. Similarly, the primary TCF set has a
         synonym, T, which refers to TCF, independent of the value of TCFNAME$.

         Table 6–1 assumes PCFNAME$ and TCFNAME$ have nonzero values. If the set-name
         referenced does not exist, the following set references automatically become SGS
         references (see 3.1.2) with a label, I, of set-name.




7831 1784–010                                                                                   6–5
Permanent and Temporary Streams




                             Table 6–1. PCF and TCF Set References


          Set Reference                       Value Returned                     Type

         [set-name]            Number of element entries in set-name. If     NUMERIC
                               set-name is empty, the value returned is 0.
         [set-name,n, 1,1]     Element name for the n th element entry in    SYMBOLIC
                               set-name. If there is no n th entry in
                               set-name, a NO FIND condition exists.
         [set-name,n, 2,1]     Version name for the n th element entry is    SYMBOLIC
                               set-name. If there is no n th entry in
                               set-name, a NO FIND condition exists.
         [set-name,n, 3,1]     Concatenated element and version name.        SYMBOLIC
                               If there is no n th entry in set-name, a NO
                               FIND condition exists.


       The order of the element entries in a PCF or TCF set, unless controlled by the O or
       Q options (see Table 2–1) is determined by the order in which they are encountered
       on input. SSG reads across the @SSG call and then down through file-id statements.

       The extended SGS subfield references described in 3.1.2.2 can be used. The existence
       of version-name for a particular element can be tested using the extended reference
       [set-name,n,2,1,LEN$]. For example, [TCF0001,5,2,1,LEN$] would be non-zero if and only
       if the fifth element in TCF set TCF0001 has a non-space version name.

       For those PCF and TCF sets that have an associated program file, the SGS file name
       references described in 3.1.2.3 can be used. To avoid ambiguity, the PCF and TCF set
       names used in the references must be different from SGS labels associated with file
       names.

       The rules for composing set references are identical to the rules for composing SGS
       references, described in 3.1.2.4.

       Set Reference Example
       This example uses the following input:
         @ssg,ik
         TCF001
         *ELTA
         -2,2
         *ELTB/VERSIONB
         -3,3
         @eof
         TCF002
         *ELTC
         *ELTD
         -22,33
         *ELTE
         -1,10
         @EOF




6–6                                                                            7831 1784–010
                                                       Permanent and Temporary Streams




         SSG logically structures the set information as follows:

           TCF0001 ELTA
           TCF0001 ELTB/VERSIONB
           TCF0002 ELTC
           TCF0002 ELTD
           TCF0002 ELTE

         Set references return the following:

           Reference                   Value

           [tcf0001]                    2
           [tcf0001,1,1,1]              ELTA
           [tcf0001,1,2,1]                         There is no version name.
           [tcf0001,1,3,1]              ELTA
           [tcf0001,2,3,1]              ELTB/VERSIONB
           [tcf0002,2,1,1]              ELTD

         Example
         The following example shows how you can use TCFNAME$ and PCFNAME$ to check
         TCF or PCF set entries for fields on the element cards.

           *SET CASE$                          .strings case insensitive
           *SET PCFNAME$                       .satisfy on PCF chain first
           *INCREMENT A TO [PCF001]
           *SET name-hold = ’[PCF001,A,3,1]’   .save the element/version-name
           *CLEAR PCFNAME$                     .search SGS chain only
           *IF COLUMN SEARCH FROM PCF001,1,1,1 FOR [*name-hold]
           *IF ROW SEARCH FROM PCF001,STMT$,1,1 FOR DELETE
           #DELETE,CS PCF.[*name-hold]
           #DELETE,CS BASE.[*name-hold]
           *ENDIF
           *ENDIF
           *SET PCFNAME$                       .back to PCF chain
           *LOOP
           *CLEAR CASE$                        .restore normal case

         This example increments through each entry in the PCF set ’PCF001’. If the keyword
         DELETE is present on the element entry, that element will be deleted from the BASE
         and PCF files.




7831 1784–010                                                                            6–7
Permanent and Temporary Streams




6.8. Line–Change Format
       TCF line change statements, relative to an already existing line change statement in
       the PCF, have the following formats:

       Format 1
         -perm-line-nos/relative-line-nos

       Format 2
         -perm-line-nos/relative-line-nos/new-line-nos

       where:

       perm-line-nos
           are the same line numbers as in the line-change image in the PCF stream being
           revised.

       relative-line-nos
           are the relative line numbers where a change is to be made to the data images
           in the PCF stream.

       For relative referencing purposes, the line-change statement itself in the PCF is
       numbered relative line 0. Data images following that line-change statement, until
       the next line-change statement in the same element entry, are numbered as relative
       lines 1,2, ..., and so forth.

       relative-line-nos can be of two forms:

           n

               indicates that any data images in the TCF following this line change statement,
               which is relative to the PCF, are to be inserted into the PCF stream after relative
               line n. Relative line n must exist in the PCF stream.

           n,m

               indicate that relative lines n through m (n <= m) of the PCF stream are to be
               removed. Data images in the TCF following this line change statement are to
               be inserted into the PCF stream after relative line m. Relative line m and,
               therefore, relative line n must exist in the PCF stream.




6–8                                                                                 7831 1784–010
                                                            Permanent and Temporary Streams




         new-line-nos can be of four forms:

             p

             p-

             p,q

             p,q-

                 where line change statements with new-line-nos specified direct SSG to insert
                 new-line-nos from the TCF into the PCF stream. The new-line-nos are inserted
                 before inserting any data images and are inserted into the PCF stream after
                 relative line n or m (where n or m depends on the type of relative-line-nos).

         Example 1
         The following example shows how line change statements and data images in a PCF
         stream are numbered for relative referencing purposes:


                                      Relative Line       PCF
                                       Numbers           Stream

                                           0            -2,3
                                            1           A
                                           2            B
                                           3            C
                                           0            -10
                                            1           XYZ
                                           0            -14,16


         Therefore, any image in a PCF stream can be referenced, first according to the
         line-change statement associated with it, and then according to its position relative
         to that line-change statement.

         Notes:
                If the first image in a PCF or TCF stream is a data image, SSG assumes the
                 existence of a -0 line-change statement (relative line 0). However, in this case,
                 a -0/0 or -0/0,0 line-change statement is in error, since the -0 line-change
                 statement does not actually exist. If relative changes of this type are
                 anticipated, it is recommended that an explicit -0 line-change statement be
                 supplied.
          If a temporary correction file stream contains -0/0 followed by images to be
         inserted at the start of the PCF stream, the PCF stream must contain a -0 as the
         first image. If the PCF stream does not contain the -0, SSG rejects the change and
         issues the message “INVALID ATTEMPT TO MAKE RELATIVE CORRECTION:….”




7831 1784–010                                                                                    6–9
Permanent and Temporary Streams




       Example 2
       This example uses the following PCF and TCF input:

        PCF                   TCF                       Revised PCF

                              -2                        -2
                              MNO                       MNO
        -4,6                  -4,6/2,2                  -4,6
        X                     LJK                       X
        YYY                                             LJK
                              -8                        -8
                              ZZZ                       ZZZ
        -10                   -10/0,0/12,12             12,12
        B                     NEW                       NEW
                                                        B
        -25,25-               -25,25-/1,1               -25,25-
        /ABC/DEF/             /ABC/EFJ/                 /ABC/EFJ/
        -32,33-               -32,33-/0,0/32,32-        -32,32
        /L/S/                 -32,33-/1/35,35-          /L/S/
        /A0/A2/                                         -35,35-
                                                        /A0/A2/
        -43,44                                          -43,44
                              -54                       -54
                              QRS                       QRS
                              -72,76                    -72,76

       The changes in the TCF stream reference the PCF as follows:

        -2
        MNO

       Since this is not a relative change to the PCF, it must be a change to be added to
       the PCF. Therefore, -2 and MNO are inserted at the beginning of the revised PCF.

        -4,6/2,2
        LDK

       Deletes line 2 relative to the image -4,6 in the PCF, which is YYY, and inserts the
       line LJK at that position.

        -8
        ZZZ




6–10                                                                             7831 1784–010
                                                        Permanent and Temporary Streams




         Is a correction to be added to the PCF. Therefore, -8 and ZZZ are inserted
         between -4,b and -12,12 followed by the line NEW.

          -10/0,0/12,12
          NEW

         Deletes line 0 relative to the image -10 in the PCF, which is -10, and inserts the
         image -12,12 followed by the line NEW.

          -25,25/1,1
          /ABC/EFJ/

         Deletes line 1 relative to the image -25,25- in the PCF, which is /ABC/DEF/, and inserts
         the line /ABC/EFJ/ at that position.

          -32,33-/0,0/32,32-

         Deletes line 0 relative to the image -32,33- in the PCF, which is -32,33-, and inserts the
         image -32,32-.

          -32,33-/1/35,35-

         Inserts the image -35,35- following line 1 relative to the image -32,33- in the PCF, which
         is /L/S/.

          -54
          QRS

         Is a correction to be added; -54 and QRS are inserted between -43,44 and -72,76 in the
         revised PCF.


6.9. Change Control Characters
         The standard control character for the *CORRECT and *MERGE types of merges
         is the minus sign ( - ). You can change this standard to another control image (up to
         three characters), according to the following format:

          -=new-control-characters

         Note: Control characters cannot be numeric. Bracketed references are not allowed.
         The image must appear as the first image in the stream to which it belongs.

         When you perform a *CORRECT merge, the change of control character image can
         appear as the first image of the PCF stream, as the first image of the TCF stream, or
         immediately after the *CORRECT directive in the skeleton. Any other images, in either
         stream or between the *CORRECT directive and its matching *ENDCORRECT directive,
         are in error. Images in the skeleton that look like change control character images and
         are not between a *CORRECT directive and its matching *ENDCORRECT directive are
         treated as nondirective images and do not affect the control character.




7831 1784–010                                                                                  6–11
Permanent and Temporary Streams




           If the change control character image is the first image in the PCF stream, it
            triggers the change and the image is passed to the revised PCF stream (if there is
            one). It does not appear in the generated output stream.
           If the change control character image is in the TCF stream, it triggers the change
            and the image is passed to the revised temporary correction (RTC) stream (if there
            is one), but is not passed to the revised PCF stream or the generated output
            stream.
           If the change control character image appears after the *CORRECT directive in
            the skeleton, it triggers the change but does not appear in the revised PCF, RTC,
            or generated output streams.
       Note: When listing the RTC stream (@SSG,H), SSG does not recognize a change of
       the change control characters (-=xxx) in the RTC stream. This causes the subsequent
       counter to be incorrect.

       When a change control character image for a *CORRECT merge comes from more
       than one source, the destination for each image is as described in the preceding
       paragraph. However, the new-control-characters for that merge are determined
       according to the following priorities:

       1.   TCF overrides PCF and skeleton.
       2.   PCF overrides skeleton.

       After each *CORRECT merge is performed (the matching *ENDCORRECT directive is
       encountered), the control character is reset to -.

       When you perform a *MERGE merge, the change control character image can appear
       as the first image of either input TCF stream. If a change control character image
       appears in both TCF streams, the one in TCFxxxx is used. This image triggers the
       change of control characters and is sent to TCFzzzz (TCFyyyy, if assumed) but does
       not appear in the generated output stream. When the merge is complete, the control
       character is reset to -.


6.10. Revised Temporary Correction (RTC) Stream
       The revised temporary correction (RTC) stream is a collection of element entries
       created by SSG. The RTC stream is saved in param-4 if it is specified on the @SSG call.
       The RTC stream is listed if the H option is specified on the @SSG call. If either param-4
       or the H option is specified on the @SSG call, the value of the reserved global variable,
       RTC$, is initially set to 1 by SSG; otherwise, it is initially set to 0.

       An RTC entry is created by SSG when

           A permanent stream and a temporary stream are merged with the *CORRECT
            directive. If a temporary stream is not included in the merge (due to the absence
            of the input temporary stream or specification of PERM on the *CORRECT
            directive), an RTC entry is not created.
           Either the H option or param-4, or both, are specified on the @SSG call. If neither
            param-4 nor the H option is specified on the @SSG call, no RTC images are
            created.


6–12                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                        Permanent and Temporary Streams




              The value of the reserved global variable RTC$ is nonzero when the *CORRECT
               directive is encountered. Creation of RTC images results only if the value of RTC$
               is nonzero when the *CORRECT directive is encountered.

         RTC$ can be used two ways:

              To alert the skeleton that an RTC stream is being maintained by SSG.
              To turn the generation of RTC images off or on when a *CORRECT operation is
               being performed.

         An RTC entry is a copy of the temporary stream with line numbers revised to make it
         applicable to the updated symbolic element created by applying the permanent
         stream to the original base level symbolic element. If skeleton-generated corrections
         are used, the revised line numbers reflect skeleton-generated corrections. Skeleton-
         generated corrections are included in the output of the *CORRECT directive. They
         modify the new base level symbolic element.

         Example 1
                                                                                 Base
             Base             PCF            TCF            PCF+TCF            +PCF+TCF

             B image 1        -1,2          -6,9          -1,2                 B image 3
             B image 2        -4            -12,12        -4                   B image 4
             B image 3        P image a     T image a      P image a           P image a
             B image 4        -10,10        T image b     -6,9                 B image 5
             B image 5                                    -10,10               B image 11
             B image 6                                    -12,12               T image a
             B image 7                                    T image a            T image b
             B image 8                                    T image b
             B image 9
             B image 10
             B image 11
             B image 12

             Newbase                        RTC                            Newbase+RTC

             B image 3                      -5,8                            B image 3
             B image 4                      -10,10                          B image 4
             P image a                      T image a                       P image a
             B image 5                      T image b                       B image 5
             B image 6                                                      B image 11
             B image 7                                                      T image a
             B image 8                                                      T image b
             B image 9
             B image 11
             B image 12




7831 1784–010                                                                                6–13
Permanent and Temporary Streams




       If the temporary stream contains line-correction images that change line-correction
       images in the permanent stream (for example, -1,2/0,0/2,3), it is impossible to be sure
       that the RTC stream will be correct. It is also impossible to restore lines deleted by the
       original permanent stream. Examine the RTC stream before you apply it to the
       updated symbolic element. SSG places the following message around the line-
       correction images that may be in error:

        *WARNING *.******UPDATE PERFORMED-RESULTS ARE UNPREDICTABLE******

       Example 2
       This example uses the following permanent and temporary input streams:

        PCF              TCF

        -1,2            -3,3
        -6,8            -6,8/0,0/6
                        -16

       The RTC stream looks like the following:

         -1,1
         *WARNING *.****** UPDATE PERFORMED-RESULTS ARE UNPREDICTABLE ******
         -3
         *WARNING *.****** UPDATE PERFORMED-RESULTS ARE UNPREDICTABLE ******
         -11

       The TCF stream revises the PCF stream by deleting lines 6,7, and 8 in the original
       symbolic element and inserting images after line 6. After the PCF has been applied
       to the symbolic element, SSG cannot restore the deleted images, so the RTC stream
       needs to be modified to produce the desired results. The following message points
       to the images in the RTC stream that may need to be further revised (in this case, -3):

        "UPDATE PERFORMED-RESULTS ARE UNPREDICTABLE"

       When revised images are generated from input temporary images and those images
       contain images with slashes ( / ), the revised images cannot be assumed to be correct.
       If the value of IMGLEN$ is not sufficient to hold the revised line number, the result is
       meaningless. Setting the value of the IMGLEN$ to a large number, such as 132, should
       eliminate any problems. This is also true for virtual line numbers produced as a result
       of the *MERGE,R directive.

Appendix A
Diagnostic, Error, and Warning
Messages

         This appendix lists and explains diagnostic, error, and warning messages.


A.1. Diagnostic Messages
           **** ABORT ENCOUNTERED AT LINE n OF source

            This message is printed when an ABORT directive is encountered. The parameter
            n indicates the line where the abort was encountered and source indicates SKEL$
            if the abort was in the main skeleton, or a define packet main if the abort was
            encountered in a define packet.

           END SSG.    ERRORS: w WARNING s SYNTAX n NO-FIND m MERGE/CORRECT
             LINES:    i INTERPRETED o OUTPUT TIME: t STORAGE: a/b/c/d/e

            The SSG termination line displays when SSG terminates normally.

            where:

            w
                is the warning.

            s
                is the value of ERRCNT$.

            n
                is the value of NOFND$.

            m
                is the value of CONFLC$.

            i
                is the value of SKELINECNT$.

            o
                is the value of GOSCNT$.



7831 1784–010                                                                           A–1
Diagnostic, Error, and Warning Messages




           t
                 is the time in standard units of processing (SUP) in the format hh:mm:ss.sss.

           a/b/c/d/e
                 describe memory allocation as follows:

                  a     is the number of times a memory packet was requested.
                  b     equals the number of errors encountered while returning packets
                        back to the available lists (should be 0).
                  c     is the number of data banks acquired during processing.
                  d     is the upper address limit in the final data bank. Data bank (D-bank)
                        addresses begin at the end of the shared I-bank.
                  e     is the maximum size of the data banks expressed as a decimal.
                        Note that the maximum number of SGSs with the same label
                        is e - 16.

                 For more information, refer to the Exec System Software Administration
                 Reference Manual (7831 0323).


A.2. Error Messages
          *ERROR* - 1 LEFT PARENTHESIS IS UNMATCHED

               With NEW_BOOLEAN$ nonzero, there was a left parenthesis that did not have
               the required matching right parenthesis on an *IF directive.

          *ERROR* - AREAD$ IMAGE GREATER THAN 132 CHAR MAX ALLOWED - PART OF SSG
          D-BANK DESTROYED

               Part of SSG has been overwritten. This happens when an image in the runstream
               is bigger than 132 characters.

          *ERROR* - BANK$ TO ACQUIRE ADDITIONAL BANK FAILED - SNAP$ OF PKT
          FOLLOWS:

               A fatal error occurred within SSG while trying to get an extra data bank in order
               to continue processing. Contact your local Unisys Support Representative.

          *ERROR* - BRK SPEC --> message

               Result of a *COPY error. See the “*ERROR* - SPEC nn -->” message
               description.

           *ERROR* - CONFLICT IN RELATIVE CORRECTIONS: *MERGE image

                During the merging of temporary streams with *MERGE, internal conflicts in line
                corrections resulted. Some conflicts could not be resolved (PCF entry unknown).
                As a result, SSG displayed this message and discarded the correction from
                TCFyyyy. The parameter image is a copy of the *MERGE directive in error.

           *ERROR* - *CORRECT DIRECTIVE MISSING MATCHING *ENDCORRECT

                A *CORRECT directive was found but no *ENDCORRECT was found to match it.

           *ERROR* - *CREATE IMAGE EXCEEDED CURRENT MAX ALLOWED

                A *CREATE image exceeded the maximum number of characters for that image
                defined by the value of IMGLEN$.

           *ERROR* - DATAFILE SPECIFIED ON DIRECTIVE LINE IS A PROGRAM FILE

                A data file was specified on the *SAVE directive line; however, this file is a
                program file. The *SAVE directive will not be executed, to prevent destroying a
                program file by rewriting it as a data file.

           *ERROR* - *DEFINE DIRECTIVE MISSING MATCHING *ENDDEFINE

                No *ENDDEFINE was found for a *DEFINE directive.

          *ERROR* - ERROR IN REVISED SKEL - SSG WILL NOT PROCESS IT

            A *CORRECT operation is performed and a *BRKPT or *MERGE directive is
            encountered. The *BRKPT or *MERGE directive is in error and is not processed.

            define-name on a *DEFINE directive is in error.

          *ERROR* - FOLLOWING DIRECTIVE OUT OF SEQUENCE

            The indicated directive is illegally nested. *DEFINE directives must precede the
            control section and may not be statically nested. *BRKPT and *MERGE directives
            cannot be embedded between a *CORRECT and its matching *ENDCORRECT.

            specified or at least one is numeric.

            not performed, and the CSFSTA$ variable and SGS remain unchanged.

                An *ELSEIF, *ELSE, *END, *ENDDEFINE, *ENDIF, *ENDCORRECT, or *LOOP
                directive was encountered in the revised skeleton that did not have a matching
                *DEFINE, *IF, *CORRECT, or *INCREMENT directive.

           *ERROR* - NON-NUMERIC CHARACTER SUBFIELD ENCOUNTERED WITH X-OPTION SORT
           SGS LABEL: label SUBFIELD VALUE: non-numeric-character

                A *SORT,X directive was encountered in the revised skeleton, indicating that a
                numeric sort is to be executed. However, a nonnumeric character was present
                on the label SGS with subfield non-numeric-character, so the sort is not
                performed.

                A program file was specified on the *SAVE directive line. Since this is a data file,

           *ERROR* - RESTORE FILE/ELEMENT IS UNOBTAINABLE, SKELETON LINE x

                The file/element specified on skeleton line x is unobtainable. The *RESTORE
                operation is not performed, and skeleton execution halts.

           *ERROR* - RESTORE FILE/ELEMENT NOT CREATED BY SSG, SKELETON LINE x

                The file/element specified on skeleton line x was not created by the SSG *SAVE
                directive. The RESTORE operation is not performed, and skeleton execution halts.

           *ERROR* - RESTORE FILE/ELEMENT TEXT IS INCOMPLETE, SKELETON LINE sl

                The file/element specified on skeleton line sl is not complete. The most likely
                cause is that the file/element contains a large number of SGSs and an SSG with
                level earlier than 24R2B was used to write it to a program file element.

                The file/element specified on skeleton line sl has an illegal version id. Currently,

          *ERROR* - RIGHT PARENTHESIS AT LINE sl, COLUMN c WITH NESTING LEVEL AT 0

            With NEW_BOOLEAN$ at nonzero, a right parenthesis was encountered on an

            number sl and column c are provided to help locate the right parenthesis that is
            in error.

          *ERROR* - RQST SIZE OF n EXCEEDS MAX BANK SIZE OF m
          *ERROR* - IMAGE LINE j FOLLOWS (RQST CALLED AT ADDRESS addr):

            A fatal error occurred within SSG where a routine was trying to acquire a
            memory packet larger than any single data bank can supply. n is the size of the
            requested memory packet, while m is the largest size available to any data bank.
            Contact your local Unisys Support Representative.

          *ERROR* - SEQUENCE OR FORMAT ERROR ON FOLLOWING IMAGE

            This message refers to either the permanent or temporary streams or both that
            are being merged at the time (*MERGE or *CORRECT). Examine the image and
            the correction stream to determine if the line is out of sequence or in the wrong
            format.

          *ERROR* - REACHED SSG MEMORY LIMIT OF 0777777 IN ALL n DATA BANKS

            The maximum address space of 262K has been reached in all available data
            banks, so SSG cannot continue. Either partition the application, *REMOVE
            entities no longer needed, or recompile SSG with more data banks by increasing
            MAXBNKS in SSG$DEF (see 1.3.2).

          *ERROR* - SPEC nn --> message

            The error has occurred for the reason specified in the message, where nn is
            the parameter field number on the @SSG call param-nn (the SPEC nn part of this
            message could be replaced by FILE ID -> or BRK SPEC ->), and message can be
            any of the following:

          BAD LABEL IMAGE

                  The label image of a system data format (SDF) input source is invalid.

          FILE EMPTY

                  SSG expected input from a file and it was empty.

          INVALID FORM ccccc

                  The param-7 input stream type is illegal. The first five characters (ccccc) of
                  the illegal type are shown. The type is not OPT, PCFxxxx, SGS, or TCFxxxx.

                with FURPUR commands.

                 The @CHG,E or @CHG,ET commands can be used to create an LEPF.

          *ERROR* - THE MAXIMUM NUMBER OF SGSS WITH THE SAME LABEL
                            (nnnnnn) HAS BEEN EXCEEDED FOR LABEL sgs-label

            The number of SGS sgs-label exceeded the maximum decimal number nnnnnn.
            The maximum number is not guaranteed to be the same for all SSG levels or
            when SSG corrections or local code are applied. The maximum number of SSGs
            with the same label can be computed from the SSG termination line STORAGE
            field, e subfield (see A.1).

            Many techniques are available for reducing the number of SGSs with the same
            label. For example, you can

             Divide the SGSs into groups with two or more different labels.
             Process the SGSs in groups of nnnnnn or less. When processing of the
               group is complete, use the *REMOVE directive to remove them and the
               *ADD directive to add the next group.
             Reformat the SGSs to reduce the number of instances. For example, you
               can increase the number of fields and subfields, which are not limited.
