Appendix B
Maintaining a Permanent TCF


B.1. Introduction
         Most software supplied by Unisys is maintained with a set of base level symbolic
         elements and a permanent correction file (PCF). The PCF is a set of changes that must
         be applied to the current base level symbolic elements to build the software product.
         A change to a product is usually in the form of a temporary correction file (TCF). The
         TCF is applied to the PCF, resulting in a new PCF, which is then applied to the base
         level symbolic elements in an incremental update. When the PCF (containing fixes
         and enhancements) becomes too large or meets the specifications for a new release,
         the following steps are taken:

         1.   The PCF is applied to the base level symbolic elements.
         2.   New base level symbolic elements are created.
         3.   The PCF is erased.

         This is called a symbolic update. Stability updates and most feature releases are
         incremental updates to base level symbolic elements.


B.2. Building a Software Product
         The building of a software product is referred to as system generation. An SSG
         skeleton and a runstream are usually supplied to help generate a runstream that
         modifies the product. The System Processor SSG Skeleton (SYSKEL) is used for
         most system processors; the basic runstream is SSGCALL.

         There are two types of changes that a user can apply during a build:

             Changes supplied by Unisys
              These include the PCF supplied on the release tape and any changes supplied by
              Unisys, such as User Communication Form (UCF) fixes.

             Changes supplied locally
              These include such items as adjusting configuration tags, modifying algorithms,
              and adding new features.




7831 1784–010                                                                                   B–1
Maintaining a Permanent TCF




        The Unisys PCF should be permanently updated only with a Unisys TCF. If local
        changes are permanently merged into the Unisys PCF, the line numbers of changes
        subsequently supplied by Unisys could cause conflicts with line numbers in the PCF.
        This can be avoided by using a two-phase system generation process to maintain a
        permanent TCF containing local changes.


B.3. Correction Files (Definitions and Relationships)
        The term two-phase system generation is used since two distinct @SSG calls may
        be required to properly maintain PCF, TCF, and the working PCF. This applies to
        most system generation runstreams. For SYSKEL users, however, the concept of
        a permanent TCF is assumed and only one @SSG call (SSGCALLT) is needed. The
        following illustration shows phases 1 and 2:




        The process shown in the figure can be summarized as follows:

          PCF   <- PCF + TCF    (Phase 1a)
          TCFP <- TCFP + TCFT    (Phase 1b)
          WORKING PCF  <- TCFP + PCF    (Phase 2)

        where:

        PCF
        is the PCF created by Unisys and initially supplied on the release tape. It is updated
        locally by applying the TCF, resulting in a new PCF. This new PCF should probably be
        saved (before phase 2 if SYSKEL is not used) since it replaces the PCF originally
        supplied by Unisys.




B–2                                                                               7831 1784–010
                                                                  Maintaining a Permanent TCF




         TCF
         is the TCF supplied by Unisys, typically in answer to a UCF. It is merged locally with the
         current PCF, resulting in a new PCF. The line numbers in the TCF are relative to the
         Unisys working PCF.

         TCFP
         is the permanent TCF. It contains local modifications to PCF and is modified by
         the TCFT. Initially, TCFP is empty or is an SSG revised temporary correction (RTC)
         stream based on the old TCFP and the old PCF (OLDPCF$ for SYSKEL users). The old
         PCF was used by Unisys to perform the last symbolic update.

         TCFT
         is the temporary TCF. It is merged locally with the current TCFP, resulting in
         a new TCFP. The line numbers in the TCFT are relative to the working PCF.

         WORKING PCF
         is the result of merging the TCFP with the PCF when the system generation is
         performed. The working PCF need not be saved, just listed. This can be done either by
         using the R or J options on the @SSG call (if the skeleton saves the working PCF) or by
         using the V or W options on the generated processor calls.


B.4. Local Use of a Permanent TCF
         Although different software products use different system generation procedures,
         the following steps outline the basic procedure for a typical runstream:

         1.    Initialize all user files. For SYSKEL users, this is a call on the System Processor
               File Utility SSG Skeleton (INITIAL).
         2.    Create the new PCF and the new TCFP. This can be accomplished in the same
               SSG skeleton. New TCFT entries can modify new TCF entries. For SYSKEL users,
               this is automatically done by adding (@ADD) TPF$ element SSGCALLT (rather than
               SSGCALL) in step 4.
         3.    Save the new PCF. This can automatically be included in the skeleton from step 2
               by initially testing for any (primary) TCF entries. Also, to allow phase 2 to correctly
               select elements (for example, to assemble and compile) you must define a
               scheme to remember which elements were modified by phase 1. For SYSKEL
               users, this is automatically done in step 4.
         4. Perform the system generation. For SYSKEL users, this is done by adding (@ADD)
            TPF$ element SSGCALLT. The presence of SSGCALLT (rather than SSGCALL)
            informs SYSKEL that a permanent TCF exists.
         5.     Save the results of step 4, the updated version of the software product. If the
                new TCFP is not cataloged or was not saved in step 3, it should be saved now.
                For SYSKEL users, this is automatic if the appropriate SYSKEL directives have
                been supplied; if not, INITIAL can now be used to save any files. The revised
                PCF from step 4 need not be saved, since all that is needed is the listing of the
                revised (working) PCF.




7831 1784–010                                                                                        B–3
Maintaining a Permanent TCF




        The new software product is now built. The new Unisys PCF has been saved, along
        with the new TCFP. The working PCF is contained in the listings supplied by step 4.


B.5. A Sample Skeleton
        The following example is a modified version of an SSG E-option listing of a skeleton
        capable of performing phase 1 of a two-phase system generation. Input to this
        skeleton consists of the PCF, TCF, TCFP, TCFT, and any number of secondary TCF
        sets TCF1,...,TCFn and TCFT1,...,TCFTm. (The numbers n and m both have a
        maximum value of 999 when using this skeleton.) Each TCF set input is merged
        with itself to remove duplicate element entries.

        A UCF response usually consists of a sequence of changes. A change contains code
        to be introduced into the PCF. A change also contains control information to help in
        determining how to merge that code into the PCF. Attached to each change is a
        system number. Placing all changes with the lowest system number in TCF1, then
        all changes with the next highest system number in TCF2, and so on, causes changes
        to be properly merged into PCF (by this skeleton). Similar facilities are provided for
        large scale local modification by entering TCFT1,...,TCFTm.

        Example

        The following changes would be assigned TCF sets as follows:


              Change              System             TCF Set
              Number              Number

                 123               12.2.1              TCF1
                 124               12.2.1              TCF1

                 157               12.2.3              TCF2


                 234               12.2.5              TCF3
                 235               12.2.5              TCF3
                 236               12.2.5              TCF3

                 345               12.2.10             TCF4




B–4                                                                              7831 1784–010
                                                              Maintaining a Permanent TCF




         Runstream
         A possible runstream:

           @SSG,PT SKEL,,,,,,PCF,PCF.,TCFP,TCFP.
              TCF1 chg123
              TCF1 chg124
              TCF2 chg157
              TCF3 chg234
              TCF3 chg235
              TCF3 chg236
              TCF4 chg345
           @EOF



         Skeleton
                SSG   REVISED SKELETON

         0001    00   *. This skeleton maintains PCF and TCFP.
         0002    00   *.
         0003    00   *. PCF must be a P option PCF, and TCFP must be a T option TCF.
         0004    00   *.
         0005    00   *. PCF is modified by TCF1,...,TCF999 and TCF; TCFP
                         by.TCFT1,...,TCFT999 and TCFT.
         0006    00   *.
         0007    00   *. Only PCF modification is described, since TCFP modification
                         is analogous:
         0008    00   *.
         0009    00   *.   1. Transfer TCF to TCFU. This is accomplished by
                              Merge_duplicates.
         0010    00   *. All duplicate element entries are “straight“ merged (*MERGE, no
         0011    00   *. R option, not a “relative“ merge) into TCFU. TCFU then acts as
         0012    00   *. a catch all in step 3.
         0013    00   *.
         0014    00   *.   2. Each TCFi is transferred (*MERGE) to TCFUi by
                               Merge_duplicates.
         0015    00   *.
         0016    00   *. The TCFU[i] sets and element entries now look something like:
         0017    00   *.
         0018    00   *. (All elements in all TCFU[i] sets)
         0019    00   *.       E1/V1 E2/V2 E3/V3 E4/V4 E5/V5 E6/V6 E7/V7 ...
         0020    00   *. TCFU1 Yes     Yes         Yes
         0021    00   *. TCFU2 Yes     Yes   Yes   Yes

         Comments
         1.    The S option on the @SSG call is not always used. If used, it must be in
               conjunction with the X option. Otherwise, SET should be modified (for
               example, use subfields 2 and 3).
         2.    Some skeletons ignore version names on element entries (for example, SYSKEL
               level 5R1). This skeleton does not; for example, entry A is treated as an entry
               different from A/B.
         3.    This skeleton does not assume sorted TCF input. A sorted TCF is desirable.
               If sorted, the X option should be used for library elements. If TCFs are sorted,
               the user must merge changes with the same system number prior to using this
               skeleton. A TCF set is assigned to each system number. The skeleton first
               merges (*MERGE) changes with the same system number, (TCF1 with TCF1
               giving TCFU1, TCF2 with TCF2 giving TCFU2, ...) then merges (*MERGE,R)
               system numbers (TCFU2 with TCFU1 giving TCFU2, TCFU3 with TCFU2 giving
               TCFU3, ...).

         Note: SKEL assumes FURPUR level 28R1 or later with the Z option on @PACK
         images.

Appendix C
Examples

         This appendix gives examples for the use of SSG.


C.1. Introduction
         SSG is well suited to perform

             Complex, demanding tasks that require a significant design phase
             Quick, spur-of-the-moment data generation

         Both types of applications use symbolic streams (SGS streams) and a skeleton
         to direct the generation of one or more symbolic streams.

         An SGS stream and a skeleton stream are required to perform a particular task,
         so you must build a structure (made of SGSs) and an interpreter (skeleton).

         To design the structure, you must be familiar with

             Allowable SGS formats
             Entities already in SGS format
             The ease with which a symbolic stream can be edited to make it look like an
              SGS stream
             Features built into SSG to ease referencing the structure

         Typically, tasks involving program files are easy to implement with SSG since SSG
         communicates directly with the operating system. To build an SGS stream based on
         elements in a program file, place filename./label on the @SSG call or on an SGS file-id.

         In other cases, symbolic streams are already in SGS format or require a minor change.
         The following sequence shows a trial attempt at making a symbolic stream appear to
         SSG as a format with the label label:

             @ED,N current-symbolic-stream,SGS-formatted-symbolic-stream
             Change /./’’.’’/ All
             Change /;/’’;’’/ All
             Change //label / Rep
             @SSG some skeleton stream,SGS-formatted-symbolic-stream
             @EOF




7831 1784–010                                                                                 C–1
Examples




       Depending on how long you need it, skeleton design can be

           Ignored (use the skeleton once, possibly edit the generated output stream)
           Given careful attention (save the skeleton for reuse)
           Designed by task force (document, distribute to users)

       Note: Some of the examples shown in this appendix can be found in the utility file
       of the SSG release tape.


C.2. Tic Tac Toe Example
       The following Tic Tac Toe example allows interaction with the skeleton, with flow of
       control in the skeleton controlled by input. Error checking is done on the input. If
       the input is incorrect, it is resolicited. This skeleton is intended to work on UTS 200,
       UTS 400, and UTS 20 terminals. It makes extensive use of string variables, including
       nonprintable ASCII characters.

         Skeleton Analysis by Line Number
         5.
                The variable pos11 is set to a string of nonprintable ASCII characters. These are
                the cursor positioning sequence (when combined with the value of SI) for move
                1,1. The cursor positioning sequence is ESC (033) UT (013) row (040) column (056)
                and SI (017).

         30.
                STRINGCHAR$ is set to a comma. This will be used later by the functions
                BEFORE_STRING$ and AFTER_STRING$.

         54.
                The cursor is positioned at row 1, column 9 to start printing the first vertical grid
                line.

         93.
                LP is incremented to 1000. This allows control to be transferred to the top of the
                loop if the input is not valid.

         94.
                The next move is solicited. The expected format is R,C.

         96.
                If a control statement was input, control is returned to the statement after the call
                to this define packet.

         106.
                If the move was not a valid move, the next iteration of the loop takes place. The
                move is requested again.

         135.




7831 1784–010                                                                                      C–11
Examples




              Escape mode on output is entered to allow the program to perform all screen
              handling functions.

       149.
              The define packet ’OPPONENT_MOVE’ is called. This line has the same affect as
              ’*PROCESS opponent_move’.

       171.
              The cursor is positioned to line 23, column 18, and the message ’I win, thanks for
              the game’ is printed.

       370.
              Board position 1,2 is checked to see if the user put a mark there. If so, a ’O’ is
              drawn at position 3,2.


C.3. File Lister Example
       The following example is an interactive skeleton that allows all the symbolic elements
       in a program file to be listed with an @PRT,S directive. The skeleton asks for the file
       names to be listed, asks for the number of copies to be printed, and asks where the
       listing is to be printed.

           SSG       REVISED SKELETON

            1.      0    *display .
            2.      0    *display ’     Program file lister’
            3.      0    *display .
            4.      0    *set stringchar$ = ’.’
            5.      0    *clear iopt$
            6.      0    #cat,p brk$pr$$(+1).,///3000
            7.      0    #brkpt print$/brk$pr$$
            8.      0    *.
            9.      0    *increment a$$ to 1000
           10.      1    | *.
           11.      1    | *remove sgs element$
           12.      1    | *accept,e ans ’Program file to list <end>’
           13.      1    | *.
           14.      1    | *if +eof$ <> 0
           15.      2    | | *display ’Lister cancelled’
           16.      2    | | *display .
           17.      2    | | *display ’End file lister’
           18.      2    | | *clear add_gos$
           19.      2    | | *return
           20.      1    | *endif
           21.      1    | *.
           22.      1    | *if +[*ans,length$]=0
           23.      2    | | *exit
           24.      1    | *endif
           25.      1    | *.
           26.      1    | *set ans = ’[*ans,before_string$]’       . make sure there



C–12                                                                                  7831 1784–010
                                                                         Examples




                       is no period
           27.   1   | *csf @asg,az [*ans]                  . does the file exist?
           28.   1   | *if +csfsta$<0
           29.   2   | | *display ’Unable to assign file ’’[*ans].’’ - listing not
                          done’
           30.   2   | | *iterate a$$
           31.   1   | *endif
           32.   1   | *.
           33.   1   | *add sgs [*ans]./element$         . add the TOC as SGSs
           34.   1   | *sort,l sgs element$           . sort, including version
           35.   1   | *increment elt to [element$]
           36.   2   | | *if [element$,elt,3,1] <= 4
           37.   3   | | | #hdg,p       [element$,elt,7,1].[element$,elt,6,1]
           38.   3   | | | #prt,s [element$,elt,7,1].[element$,elt,6,1]
           39.   2   | | *endif
           40.   1   | *loop
           41.   1   | *.
           42.   0   *loop
           43.   0   *.
           44.   0   #brkpt print$
           45.   0   *accept,e copies ’Number of copies to print <none> ? ’
           46.   0   *if eof$ is set
           47.   1   | *display ’Lister cancelled’
           48.   1   | *clear add_gos$
           49.   0   *else
           50.   1   | *.
           51.   1   | *if +[*copies,numeric_length$]>0
           52.   2   | | *accept,e device ’Printer <PR> ? ’
           53.   1   | *endif
           54.   1   | *if eof$ is set
           55.   2   | | *display ’Lister cancelled’
           56.   2   | | *clear add_gos$
           57.   2   | | *return
           58.   1   | *else
           59.   2   | | *.
           60.   2   | | *if +[*copies,numeric_length$]>0
           61.   3   | | | #sym brk$pr$$.,[*copies],[*device]
           62.   3   | | | *display ’Print will be sym’’ed to [*device] by
                            [info$,1,1,2]’
           63.   2   | | *endif
           64.   1   | *endif
           65.   0   *endif
           66.   0   *.
           67.   0   *display .
           68.   0   *display ’End file lister’

       Skeleton Analysis by Line Number
       5.
             Suppress printing of SGSs that are added.

       9.
             A$$ is incremented to 1000. This permits the question to be asked repeatedly.

       18.
             If a control statement is input, ADD_GOS$ is set to 0. This causes the generated
             output stream not to be added (@ADD).

       19.
             If a control statement is input, the skeleton is terminated at this point.

       33.
             The TOC of the file input is passed to SSG as SGSs with the label ELEMENT$.

       34.
             The SGSs just added are sorted across all fields so the listed elements are
             alphabetically ordered by element and version.


C.4. A Correcting Skeleton (CORRECTSKEL)
       CORRECTSKEL was written for ease of correcting (*CORRECT) multiple PCF sets.

       Features
            The value of PCFNAME$ is irrelevant.
            By varying [#6], the input permanent streams can be left unchanged. This could
             prove to be useful in conjunction with *CORRECT,P.
            The keyword ALL directs the *CORRECT directive to revise all permanent streams
             for which there is a corresponding temporary stream and allows for extensions
             using *ELSF.

       Runstream
            @SSG,P saved skeleton        ,,,,,,PCFxxxx      ,filename     .
            TCFxxxx SDF stream
            @EOF




C–14                                                                                 7831 1784–010
                                                                            Examples




         Skeleton
           SSG    REVISED SKELETON

           0001   00    *.This skeleton assumes no duplicate element entries exist
           0002   00    *.
           0003   00    *Define Correct
           0004   01       *.
           0005   01       *. *Process CORRECT <keyword> [#2] with [#4] giving [#6]
           0006   01       *.
           0007   01       *. where: <keyword> ALL All elements in [#2] are
                               "corrected" by [#4], or
           0008 01        *           Anything besides ALL, by adding *ELSF
           directives
           0009 01        *        [#2]     Any PCF set
           0010 01        *        [#4]     Any TCF set
           0011 01        *        [#6]     Any PCF set
           0012 01        *.
           0013 01        *. Verify result of using Correct, that modification is
                              remembered
           0014   01      *.
           0015   01      *If not [[#6],0,F]
           0016   02         *Message,iet Output PCF set [#6] has no program file
                              attached!
           0017   01      *End
           0018   01      *.
           0019   01      *. Process as directed by <keyword>
           0020   01      *.
           0021   01      *If [#1] = ALL
           0022   02         *.
           0023   02         *. For each element entry in the temporary stream
           0024   02         *.
           0025   02         *Increment E to [[#4]]
           0026   03            *Correct,p [[#4],E,1,1]/[[#4],E,2,1] [#2] with [#4]
                                 giving [#6]
           0027   03            *Endcorrect
           0028   02         *Loop
           0029   01      *Endif
           0030   00    *Enddefine
           0031   00    *Eject,ed

           0032   00    *. For each TCF set, "correct" the corresponding PCF set
           0033   00    *.
           0034   00    *Increment T to [$TCF]
           0035   01       *.
           0036   01       *. Only process those sets with element entries (could be
                               TCF, ...)
           0037   01       *.
           0038   01       *If [[$TCF,T,1,1]]
           0039   02          *.
           0040   02          *. Create the corresponding PCF set (TCFxxxx -> PCFxxxx)
           0041   02          *.



7831 1784–010                                                                     C–15
Examples




           0042   02       *Create SGS: PSET PCF[$TCF,T,1,1,0,4,[$TCF,T,1,1,5]-3]
           0043   02       *.
           0044   02       *. Carry out action assumed ("correct all")
           0045   02       *.
           0046   02       *Process Correct ALL [PSET,1,1,1] with [$TCF,T,1,1]
                            giving [PSET,1,1,1]
           0047 02         *.
           0048 02         *. In case the sort is on, remove PSET SGSs (always use
           index 1)
           0049 02         *.
           0050 02         *Remove SGS PSET
           0051 01       *Endif
           0052 00     *Loop


C.5. UTS Calculator Skeleton
       The following skeleton uses the ability of SSG to perform decimal, hexadecimal, octal,
       and binary arithmetic like a calculator. The answer appears in decimal, hexadecimal
       (preceded by a $ sign), octal (preceded by a 0), and binary forms. This skeleton is
       intended for use on a UTS terminal, but can be adapted to any demand terminal.

           SSG     REVISED SKELETON

            1.   0   *set clear = c’033145033115’
            2.   0   *display clear
            3.   0   *display    ’           UTS calculator’
            4.   0   *display .
            5.   0   *display    ’    Enter a number or numeric expression,
           parentheses’
            6.   0   *display    ’ are not recognized, only *, /, +, and – are
           recognized’
            7.   0   *display .
            8.   0   *display    ’ To enter a hexadecimal, octal, or binary
           number, ’
            9.   0   *display    ’ use one of the following formats: ’
           10.   0   *display .
           11.   0   *display ’    hexadecimal - h’’hex-number’’’
           12.   0   *display ’    decimal - decimal-number’
           13.   0   *display ’    octal - o’’octal-number’’’

         Skeleton Analysis by Line Number
         1.
               The variable CLEAR is set to the ASCII control sequence to clear a UTS screen.

         19.
               An ’ER APRTCN$’ is performed to cause escape mode on output.

         24.
               The variable NEXT_LINE is set to the ASCII control sequence to cause a
               <cursor-to-home> <delete-line> and then position the cursor to line 23, column 1.
               This gives the screen scrolling.

         29.
               NOFND$ is set to 101 to suppress NO FIND message from being printed.

         38.
               The expression entered is substituted into the *SET expression for evaluation.

         39.
               If a NO FIND or a SYNTAX ERROR occurred on the *SET directive, any error
               message printed by SSG is erased and replaced by the user’s message.

         44.
               The result of the expression is printed in decimal, hexadecimal, octal, and binary.

C.6. Define Packet Examples
       The following examples illustrate a set of define packets that can be used by any
       skeleton.

C.6.1. To Find a Day (Find_the_Day)
       This code converts the month/day/year part of a TDATE$ word to days from January 1,
       1964 [relative day 1, a Wednesday], including leap years, etc. It is assumed that the
       TDATE$ word [and DAYTAB are] in [main storage and can be referenced by placing
       TDATE and DAYTAB in the u-field of a referencing instruction].

       The day of the week can be found from a date using the following algorithm:

                           L,S3         A0,TDATE                   . YEAR
                           L,S1         A1,TDATE                   . MONTH
                           MSI,U        A0,365*4+1                 .
                           A            A0,DAYTAB-1,A1             .
                           SSL          A0,2                       .
                           A,S2         A0,TDATE                   . DAY

                   DAYTAB +             0*4+3                      . JAN
                          +             31*4+3                     . FEB
                          +             59*4+4                     .
                          +             90*4+4                     .
                          +             120*4+4                    .
                          +             151*4+4                    .
                          +             181*4+4                    .
                          +             212*4+4                    .
                          +             243*4+4                    .
                          +             273*4+4                    .
                          +             304*4+4                    .
                          +             334*4+4                    . DEC

       Using this algorithm, a define packet such as the following can be produced that
       converts a MMDDYY-formatted date to the day of the week.

       Features
          The algorithm requires the use of MMDDYY_Tab (DAYTAB) and MMDDYY_Day.
           If these SGSs are not defined when MMDDYY_to_Day is called, they are created.
          The algorithm is a faithful reproduction of the assembly language code with the
           following change:

              All multiplications and additions have been evaluated.

          Rather than day being placed seven times in MMDDYY_Day, it is appended to the
           created Day SGS. Although this saves only a small amount of main storage, it is,
           nevertheless, an efficient use of SSG.
          Process parameter reference method 2 is used in this define packet. This requires
           the global variable NEW_PROCESS$ to be set before calling Find_the_day.


C–18                                                                           7831 1784–010
                                                                                       Examples




             Since YY does not contain the 64 offset, the 64 offset is accounted for in the
              define packet. Note that, instead of subtracting 64 from YY at skeleton line 23,
              since 64*1461 produces a remainder of 3 at the end of the computation,
              MMDDYY_Day has been shifted.
         Call
         To compute the day on which SSG is called, enter

             *PROCESS find_the_day [DATIME$,1,3,1]

         Define Packet
             SSG        REVISED SKELETON

                   1.     0
                         *define find_the_day
                   2.     1
                         | *.
                   3.     1
                         | *.           Call:   *PROCESS FIND_THE_DAY [datime$,1,3,1]
                   4.     1
                         | *.
                   5.     1
                         | *. The answer is returned in the string variable
                               ’DAY_OF_THE_WEEK’
                6.   1   | *.
                7.   1   | *.           [#,process_level$,1,1] is YYMMDD
                8.   1   | *.           Check that the parameter is 6 numeric digits
                9.   1   | *if +[#,process_level$,1,1,numeric_length$] <> 6
               10.   2   | | *display ’[#,process_level$,1,1] is not a valid date’
               11.   2   | | *return
               12.   1   | *endif
               13.   1   | *.
               14.   1   | *.         Create the mmddyy_table SGS if there isn’t one
               15.   1   | *if not [mmddyy_table]
               16.   2   | | *create sgs: mmddyy_table
                              3,127,240,364,484,608,728,852,976,1096,1220,1340
               17.   1   | *endif
               18.   1   | *.
               19.   1   | *.         Create the mmddyy_day SGS if there isn’t one
               20.   1   | *if not [mmddyy_day]
               21.   2   | | *create sgs: mmddyy_day
             Satur,Sun,Mon,Tues,Wednes,Thurs,Fri
               22.   1   | *endif
               23.   1   | *.
               24.   1   | *.       Pick the month, day, and year out of the string
               25.   1   | *set year = [#,process_level$,1,1,sub_string$,1,2]
               26.   1   | *set month = [#,process_level$,1,1,sub_string$,3,2]
               27.   1   | *set day = [#,process_level$,1,1,sub_string$,5,2]
               28.   1   | *.
               29.   1   | *.    Perform the computations for finding the day of the
                                 week
               30.   1   | *if +year >= 64
               31.   2   | | *set year = year - 64
               32.   1   | *else
               33.   2   | | *set year = year + 100 - 64
               34.   1   | *endif

              35.    1     | *set hold = year * 1461 + [mmddyy_table,1,1,month]
              36.    1     | *set hold = hold / 4 + day + 3
              37.    1     | *set temp = hold / 7
              38.    1     | *set hold = hold - temp * 7

C.6.2. A Debug Aid (Echo_Pars)
       When debugging a skeleton or a define packet in a skeleton, it is useful to dump
       process parameters.

       Call
           *Process Echo_Pars define name

       where define name is the name of some define packet.

       Define Packet
           SSG      REVISED SKELETON

           0001     00     *Define Echo_Pars
           0002     01     *.
           0003     01     *. [#1] is the name of some define packet
           0004     01     *.
           0005     01     *. Check each process parameter defined
           0006     01     *.
           0007     01     *Increment P to [#,[#1]]
           0008     02        *.
           0009     02        *. This process parameter exist (or is it a "no find"
                                 process parameter)?
           0010 02           *.
           0011 02           *If [#P,[#1]]
           0012 03              *Message,ti Par [*P] in define packet [#1] is [#P,[#1]]
           0013 02           *Else
           0014 03              *Message,ti Par [*P] in define packet [#1] is a "no
           find"
           0015 02           *Endif
           0016 02           *.
           0017 02           *. Pickup the next process parameter defined for [#1]
           0018 02           *.
           0019 01         *Loop
           0020 00       *Enddefine




C–20                                                                           7831 1784–010
                                                                                  Examples




C.6.3. A Recursive Define Packet (Count_Nested_Calls)
         This example illustrates a recursive define packet. Note the way the images are
         interpreted if you set the M and N options on the @SSG call. You should perform
         a breakpoint (@BRKPT).

         Skeleton
           SSG    REVISED SKELETON

           0001   00   *Define Count_Nested_Calls
           0002   01     *.
           0003   01     *. This define packet illustrates use of recursion
           0004   01     *.
           0005   01     *. [#1] is the number of times to nest calls to
                             Count_Nested_Calls
           0006   01     *.
           0007   01     *. This define packet counts down from [#1] to 1 by -1
           0008   01     *. This is accomplished by calling Count_Nested_Calls
                             with a value 1 less than [#1]
           0009   01     *. When a value of 1 is reached, no more nested calls
                             are requested
           0010   01     *. This results in the most recent call to
                             Count_Nested_Calls
                             to complete processing
           0011   01     *. This will set the value of COUNT to 1, since it is
                             initially clear
           0012   01     *. The next most recent call completes, setting COUNT
                             to 2, and so on
           0013   01     *. The final value of COUNT equals the first value of [#1]
           0014   01     *.
           0015   01     *. The variable COUNT is global (*SET creates a global
                             variable)
           0016   01     *. Therefore, when all calls are complete, COUNT equals
                             the first [#1]
           0017   01     *. Had an *INCREMENT loop been used, each call would have
                             its own local variable
           0018   01     *.
           0019   01     *. Check to see if [#1] equals 1 yet
           0020   01     *.
           0021   01     *If value of [#1] > 1
           0022   02        *.
           0023   02        *. No, nest use of Count_Nested_Calls
           0024   02        *.
           0025   02        *Process Count_Nested_Calls [#1]-1
           0026   02        *.
           0027   02        *. This line isn’t reached until the last call, when
                               [#1] equals 1
           0028   02       *. (Actually, it is skipped on the last call, reached
                               on second to last, ...)
           0029   02       *.
           0030   01     *Endif



7831 1784–010                                                                              C–21
Examples




           0031   01     *.
           0032   01      *. Begin incrementing the value of COUNT
           0033   01      *.
           0034   01      *Set COUNT to COUNT+1
           0035   01      *.
           0036   01      *. All calls to Count_Nested_Calls ultimately reach here
           0037   01      *.
           0038   00   *Enddefine
           0039   00   *.
           0040   00   *. Clear COUNT so the first-executed *SET directive doesn’t
                           produce a "no find"
           0041   00   *.
           0042   00   *Clear COUNT
           0043   00   *.
           0044   00   *. Start the process, recurse 25 times
           0045   00   *.
           0046   00   *Process Count_Nested_Calls 25
           0047   00   *.
           0048   00   *. Verify the value of COUNT is now 25!
           0049   00   *.
           0050   00   *Message,ti Recursive depth is [*COUNT] (should be 25)!

       Output
       The following message should appear in the SSG run log:

           Recursive depth is 25 (should be 25)!

C.6.4. Processing Unsolicited Input
       The following example illustrates processing unsolicited console operator input,
       described in detail in 3.2.3.4. This skeleton executes for approximately 3 minutes and
       is designed to simulate a skeleton than runs for hours and processes hundreds of
       thousands of data records.

       Skeleton
          1.      0    *. -----------------------------------------
          2.      0    *.        Check for unsolicited input
          3.      0    *. -----------------------------------------
          4.      0    *DEFINE check_unsolicited_input
          5.      1    | *.
          6.      1    | *.        Repeat while there is new unsolicited input
          7.      1    | *.
          8.      1    | *INCREMENT uns TO 9999999999 WHILE UNS_CONTROL$ IS SET
          9.      2    | | *SET save_input = UNS_INPUT$
         10.      2    | | *CLEAR UNS_CONTROL$ . Enable new unsolicited input
         11.      2    | | *.
         12.      2    | | *.        Process the entered unsolicited input
         13.      2    | | *.
         14.      2    | | *IF [*save_input,UCSTR$] = HELP
         15.      3    | | | *.
         16.      3    | | | *.        This is a request for the valid unsolicited
       keyins
         17.      3    | | | *.

            21.   3   | | | *DISPLAY,O ' STAT - Display the current record
         summary'
            22.   3   | | | *DISPLAY,O ' TERM - Request early termination'

         Skeleton Analysis by Line Number
         4 – 48
             Define packet that checks for and processes unsolicited console operator input.

         8 – 10, 46
             Directives that implement the second method of unsolicited input processing
             recommended in 3.2.3.4.

         53 – 61
             Define packet that outputs the record summary on an unsolicited input entry of
             STAT and on termination of the main processing loop.

         71 – 73
             Skeleton initialization directives that enable unsolicited input. This also illustrates
             how a skeleton can determine if the installed SSG supports unsolicited input.

         74 – 77
             Directives executed if the installed SSG does not support unsolicited input. This
             illustrates that a skeleton designed to process unsolicited input can still be
             executed with SSG 24R1 and earlier levels.

         88 – 117
             The main processing loop, with the recommended call to the unsolicited input
             check subroutine at line 92.

         96 – 99

             termination.
